On the Weighted Top-Difference Distance Axioms, Aggregation, and Approximation
Andrea Aveni, Ludovico Crippa, Giulio Principi

Abstract
. We study a family of distance functions on rankings that allow for asymmetric treatments of alternatives and consider the distinct relevance of the top and bottom positions for ordered lists. We provide a full axiomatic characterization of our distance. In doing so, we retrieve new characterizations of existing axioms and show how to effectively weaken them for our purposes. This analysis highlights the generality of our distance as it embeds many (semi)metrics previously proposed in the literature. Subsequently, we show that, notwithstanding its level of generality, our distance is still readily applicable. We apply it to preference aggregation, studying the features of the associated median voting rule. It is shown how the derived preference function satisfies many desirable features in the context of voting rules, ranging from fairness to majority and Pareto-related properties. We show how to compute consensus rankings exactly, and provide generalized Diaconis-Graham inequalities that can be leveraged to obtain approximation algorithms. Finally, we propose some truncation ideas for our distances inspired by [ 42 ] . These can be leveraged to devise a Polynomial-Time-Approximation Scheme for the corresponding rank aggregation problem.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 06A06, 06A75; Secondary 06A07, 30L05

1.
Introduction

The problem of aggregating preferences of heterogeneous individuals into a representative ranking, or rank aggregation in short, has sparked substantial interest across different research areas, such as Statistics, Economics, Computer Science, and Political Science. For instance, in Social Choice theory individuals are voters who cast their preferences over a pool of candidates: The goal of the aggregation is to identify
consensus preferences
. Statisticians also study ranking data aggregation providing probabilistic models based on weighted distances over rankings, prominent examples are the Mallows model and its generalizations (
[
43
]
,
[
41
]
,
[
50
]
). More recent instances of rank aggregation include collaborative filtering and recommender systems (e.g.,
[
48
]
), which are pervasive in modern digital platforms. Movies and shopping recommendations to users are often expressed as an ordered list of suggested films and items, produced by combining movie and item ratings supplied by other users. Further, rank aggregation problems are associated with metasearch and spam detection engines. The objective here is to combine rankings of web pages or hyperlinked documents produced by different ranking algorithms to improve the users’ searching experience (e.g.,
[
14
]
,
[
27
]
). Voting and more generally rank aggregation mechanisms have been proposed as instrument for planning and coordination in the field of
Multiagent Systems (e.g.,
[
30
]
).

Several methods of aggregation rely on distance functions that evaluate the proximity between two rankings. These functions quantify the level of cohesion among a collection of rankings, such as an electorate. Specularly, distance-based methods also allow to evaluate the degree of dispersion of opinions of a group of individuals. One of the most popular distances over rankings is the Kendall distance,
[
36
]
, which is defined as the minimum number of swaps of adjacent elements that transform one ranking into the other.

The Kendall distance has two main shortcomings. On the one hand, it does not take into account the relative importance of positions where the swaps occur. Yet, in many applications swaps at the top positions carry more dissimilarity compared to swaps at lower positions. For instance, when comparing alternative webpages’ rankings, one is often interested in the resulting traffic flow, as measured by the
click-through rates
(CTR). However, several studies (e.g.,
[
1
]
,
[
51
]
,
[
53
]
) have indicated a significant disparity in the average CTR between the top-ranked result and the second-ranked result, which is notably larger than the corresponding difference among the CTRs of lower-ranked webpages. In addition, the presence of screening costs and time constraints naturally favors top-ranked results when processing long pieces of information.

On the other hand, the Kendall distance also treats homogeneously all the alternatives over which rankings are cast. This hinders its applicability to settings where the aggregator may have preferences over the available alternatives. For example, in the presence of sponsored links or in the presence of different displaying costs for the alternatives involved, the aggregator’s preferences might play a crucial role in determining an aggregate ranking.

In this work, we introduce a class of distances, named weighted top-difference distances, that overcome these shortcomings while extending and connecting to several distances proposed in the literature. Our distance evaluates the proximity of two rankings in terms of three aspects: the difference between the maximal elements in each subset of alternatives, the size of such menu, and the relative importance of each alternative. We provide an axiomatic characterization of our class of distances. Such axiomatic foundation is built over a thorough study of betwenness axioms without any neutrality and invariance requirements. The results we provide not only lead to the full characterization of the proposed class of distances, but also highlight the implications of betweenness axioms taken singularly. The complete characterization of our distance is obtained by combining some betweenness and separability axioms, and it allows to have a better understanding of the existing axiomatizations of the Kendall distance and its weighted versions.

In addition to its generality, our distance is shown to be profitably applicable to rank aggregation problems.
Given a set of alternatives and a profile of rankings over them, we apply our distance to study the properties of the induced median aggregation correspondence. Among the most significant properties, we highlight fairness conditions, such as neutrality and various specifications of Pareto dominance, and majority-related aspects, such as the majority and Condorcet principles.

From a computational standpoint our contribution is threefold. We show how to compute in polynomial time all the distances considered in this work and provide a linear integer program for the ranking aggregation problem with polynomially many variables and constraints. We provide a set of generalized Diaconis-Graham inequalities and a unified framework to analyze them, thus obtaining approximation algorithms in the spirit of
[
26
]
and
[
27
]
. We also propose a Polynomial-Time Approximation Scheme inspired by the truncation techniques presented in
[
42
]
.

Organization of the paper

The next two sections are dedicated to the literature review and mathematical notation. In §
4
we present our distance, its axiomatic characterization and main properties. §
5
is devoted to median rank aggregation and its social choice theoretic properties. §
6
is dedicated to algorithmic and computational properties of both our distance and the induced voting rules presented in §
5
. In the Appendix we report the proofs of the results presented in the main text.

2.
Literature Review

Rankings and metrics

As it concerns distance functions over rankings seminal works in the literature are
[
36
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
9
]
, and
[
10
]
. In these papers, the authors provide different formulations and characterizations of the Kendall metric. More recent advancements involve providing weighted versions of the Kendall metric as in
[
31
]
and
[
39
]
. In particular,
[
31
]
proposed a weighted version of the Kendall metric that overcomes one of its main shortcomings, that is its indifference to the varying relevance of different positions in the rankings. Starting from the same challenge,
[
46
]
proposed a different solution, that they called the top-difference approach. Their distance evaluates the dissimilarity between any two rankings by considering the (possibly) distinct choices induced by the two rankings over all possible menus. This approach is not restricted to the case of linear orders, but extends to the larger domain of acyclic binary relations. In
[
46
]
the authors proved that when restricted to linear orders, the neutral version of their distance is a particular case of the weighted Kendall metric of
[
31
]
.

The distances we study embed both the Kendall metric and the dissimilarity semimetrics proposed by
[
46
]
and
[
31
]
. Weighted version of the Kendall metric are also advanced in
[
39
]
with a main focus on their computability. Differently from them, we provide an axiomatic foundation for the class of distances proposed in this work. As argued in §
4.4
, our axiomatization yields additional insights on their distances as well.
A different approach was taken by
[
38
]
where the author proposed a distance directly on choice correspondences rather than rankings. As long as such choice correspondences are rationalizable by binary relations this approach is analogous to the one of
[
46
]
.

Social Choice Theory

The use of distance functions for rank aggregation is well-established in social choice theory. This approach was advanced by
[
35
]
, and was later adopted by
[
8
]
,
[
22
]
, and
[
56
]
. In particular,
[
56
]
provided a full characterization of the median preference function induced by the Kendall metric (Kemeny voting rule). They show that such preference function is the only one satisfying three properties: reinforcing, neutrality, and the Condorcet principle. In §
5
we strengthen some of the results of
[
56
]
showing under which conditions the median preference function induced by our metric satisfies reinforcing, neutrality, and the Condorcet principle. In more recent papers
[
31
]
and
[
32
]
, the authors provided some results on median rank aggregation showing that some properties of the median approach proposed by
[
56
]
extend to more general distances. The use of distance functions for the rank aggregation problem is also analyzed in
[
4
]
and
[
42
]
, respectively
assent-maximizing social welfare functions
and the
unavailable candidate model
. Both models are encompassed by the class of distances advanced in this work. For a survey on distance-based methods see
[
11
]
and
[
21
]
.
Many voting rules (e.g., Borda, Dodgson, Plurality, and Kemeny) can be retrieved through the concept of
distance rationalization
(see
[
40
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
45
]
, and
[
29
]
) which is based on a consensus class (
[
47
]
and
[
3
]
), and a distance function over consensus elections. Specifically, in this framework, a candidate is deemed a winner if it is ranked first in one of the nearest (with respect to the given distance) consensus elections within the consensus class.

A closely related line of research focuses on the
Maximum Likelihood Estimator
(MLE) approach (
[
25
]
and
[
55
]
). The underlying assumptions concern the existence of a correct ranking of the alternatives (
ground truth
) of which we only have noisy realizations: the goal is to compute the MLE based on the available observations. Tight connections between the rank aggregation problem and the MLE approach have been discovered by
[
55
]
, and, more recently, strengthened by
[
20
]
and
[
17
]
. The design of voting rules that are MLEs for some noise model has been an active area of research, see for example,
[
28
]
,
[
49
]
,
[
44
]
, and
[
13
]
. For additional connections among the rationalizability framework, the MLE approach, and the rank aggregation problem, we refer the reader to Chapter
8
of
[
11
]
and references therein.

Computability

It is known that computing Kemeny rankings is NP-hard
[
5
]
, even with only four voters
[
27
]
. Bounds used in search-based methods to compute
optimal
Kemeny rankings have been proposed by
[
24
]
and
[
15
]
. The computational complexity of the Kemeny rank aggregation problem led to the subsequent study and development of approximation algorithms. We summarize some of them in the coming lines while deferring the reader to
[
11
]
for a more detailed description.
Randomly selecting a voter’s preference provides a
2
-approximation in expectation, and this procedure can be de-randomized by preserving the same approximation factor
[
2
]
. Sorting the alternatives in ascending order of Borda score results in a
5
-approximation to the Kemeny rank aggregation problem
[
23
]
. Another polynomial-time
2
-approximation algorithm can be obtained by replacing the Kemeny distance with the Spearman’s footrule distance, see the seminal works by
[
26
]
and
[
27
]
. Improved approximation factors for the Kemeny rank aggregation problem are obtained in
[
27
]
and
[
52
]
. A Polynomial-Time-Approximation Scheme appeared in
[
37
]
.

Additional evidence of the hardness concerning distance-based rank aggregation problems is provided by
[
32
]
when analyzing setwise versions of the Kendall distance. Since the Kendall and its setwise versions are a special case of the weighted top-difference distances presented in this paper, finding an aggregate ranking within the class of our distances is an NP-hard problem.

3.
Mathematical preliminaries

We report here the basic notation we employ in the next sections. We consider a set
C
of
n\geq 2
elements which can be interpreted as alternatives or candidates. Our investigation revolves around rankings over
C
represented as lists that arrange candidates based on preference, with the foremost candidate being the most favored and the ultimate candidate the least favored. We arbitrarily label the candidates with the numbers
\left[n\right]:=\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
, so that a ranking is represented by a
permutation
; we use the terms permutations and rankings interchangeably. By a permutation we mean a bijective function
\sigma:\left[n\right]\to\left[n\right]
, with
\sigma_{i}
denoting the candidate in the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
-position according to
\sigma
. Therefore, the set of all rankings over
[n]
, is the symmetric group denoted by
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. We, sometimes, write permutations as ordered lists e.g.,
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})
. For all permutations
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and alternatives
i,j\in[n]
, we write
i>_{\sigma}j
if
\sigma_{i}^{-1}<\sigma_{j}^{-1}
with
<_{\sigma}
bearing the analogous meaning. The identity permutation
(1,\ldots,n)
will be denoted by
\mathrm{I}
. Often we employ the group structure of
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and we adopt the convention that for two permutations
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,
\sigma\pi:i\mapsto\sigma_{\pi_{i}}
. For all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we denote by
\sigma^{-1}
the inverse permutation according to this product. Particularly relevant for our work are
transpositions
. For distinct
i,j\in\left[n\right]
, we denote by
t_{i,j}
the
i,j
-transposition, that is the permutation that agrees with the identity
\mathrm{I}
, but swaps
i
and
j
, formally
t_{i,j}=(1,\ldots,i-1,j,i+1,\ldots,j-1,i,j+1,\ldots,n)
. Whenever
|i-j|=1
, we refer to
t_{i,j}
as
adjacent
. It is noteworthy that, in general, for
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we have
\sigma t_{i,j}\neq t_{i,j}\sigma
. In particular,
\sigma t_{i,j}
is the permutation obtained from
\sigma
by exchanging
\sigma_{i}
with
\sigma_{j}
, while
t_{i,j}\sigma
is obtained from
\sigma
by exchanging
i
with
j
. Also we observe that for any distinct
i,j\in[n]
and
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we have that
\sigma t_{i,j}=t_{\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}}\sigma
and analogously
t_{i,j}\sigma=\sigma t_{\sigma^{-1}_{i},\sigma^{-1}_{j}}
.

To distinguish products (of permutations) on the left from products on the right we use the notation
\coprod_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{k}:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{n+1-k}
.

Given any two permutations
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we define their
inversion set
as the collection of all couples
(i,j)
on which
\sigma
and
\pi
disagree, that is

I(\sigma,\pi):=\{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}:i>_{\sigma}j\textrm{ and }i<_{\pi}j\}.

The
Kendall distance

\mathrm{d}_{K}
between
\sigma
and
\pi
is defined as the cardinality of
I(\sigma,\pi)
. Given a ranking
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and a candidate
j\in[n]
, we denote by
j^{\uparrow,\sigma}
the collection of alternatives preferred to
j
by
\sigma
, and
j^{\downarrow,\sigma}
is defined analogously. More precisely,

j^{\uparrow,\sigma}:=\left\{i\in[n]:i>_{\sigma}j\right\},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ j^{\downarrow,\sigma}:=\left\{i\in[n]:i<_{\sigma}j\right\}.

To ease the notation we denote
j^{\uparrow}:=j^{\uparrow,\mathrm{I}}
and
j^{\downarrow}:=j^{\downarrow,\mathrm{I}}
.
Given a subset of alternatives
S\subseteq C
and a ranking
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we denote by

M(S,\sigma)
and by
m(S,\sigma)
the maximum and minimum elements in
S
with respect to
\sigma
. Finally, for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi):=M(S,\sigma)\bigtriangleup M(S,\pi)

where
\bigtriangleup
denotes the symmetric difference of sets. We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the collection of subsets of
\left[n\right]
with cardinality larger or equal than
2
.

We denote
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}_{++}:=(0,\infty)
. When considering (signed) measures
\mu:2^{[n]}\to\mathbb{R}
on the finite set
[n]
, to ease notation we may write
\mu(j)
or
\mu_{j}
instead of
\mu(\{j\})
. As we work in a finite setting, with some abuse of notation, we will often identify measures as elements of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The counting measure will be denoted by
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.
We denote by
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
the set of non-negative measures over
2^{[n]}
, this set is identified with
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. The set of fully supported measures in
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
is denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{++}
, identified with
\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n}
. Moreover, we define also the following sets:

\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\geq}:=\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\forall i\neq j,\ \mu_{i}+\mu_{j}\geq 0\right\}\ \textnormal{and}\ \mathcal{M}_{n}^{>}:=\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\forall i\neq j,\ \mu_{i}+\mu_{j}>0\right\}.

4.
Distances on rankings

4.1.
Definition

Fix a measure
\mu:2^{[n]}\to\mathbb{R}
and a vector
{\bm{\beta}}=\left(\beta_{i}\right)_{i=2}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. Given a function
\mathrm{d}:\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, we call it
\left({\bm{\beta}},\mu\right)
-
top difference distance
if

(1)

\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma,\pi\right)=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|S|}\mu\left(\bigtriangleup_{S}\left(\sigma,\pi\right)\right).

In this case
\mathrm{d}
is denoted by
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
. Whenever,
\mu_{i},\beta_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
it is readily observed that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is always a semimetric. If in addition
\beta_{2}>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{++}_{n}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is also a metric (See Lemma
A.1
and Corollary
A.2
).
The subsets
S\subseteq[n]
can be interpreted as menus of alternatives. For each menu we measure through
\mu
the extent of disagreement between
\sigma,\pi
by comparing their maximal elements in that menu. This is weighted according to the size
|S|
of the menu. The choices of
{\bm{\beta}}
and
\mu
are important to determine different properties of
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
. Of particular interest are the following specifications of
{\bm{\beta}}
and
\mu
. If
\mu
is the counting measure, we denote
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
by
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}
. This corresponds to the
neutral
version of
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
where the candidates are treated symmetrically. When the relative weight of the size of the menus of candidates is constant, that is
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
we denote
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
by
\mathrm{d}^{\mu}
. When
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
, this corresponds to the semimetric proposed by
[
46
]
. Different specifications of
{\bm{\beta}}
allow to restrict distance evaluations to binary comparisons, this occurs when
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
. In this case, we denote the resulting semimetric by
\mathrm{d}_{K}^{\mu}
, while if furthermore
\mu=|\cdot|
, then it reduces to
\mathrm{d}_{K}
, the Kendall distance.

4.2.
Parameter space

In this section we answer to two basic questions. For which values of
{\bm{\beta}}
and
\mu
is
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
a semimetric, and which additional restrictions guarantee that it is also a metric?
To this end, first define the following sets

\displaystyle B^{\geq}_{n}:=\{{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}:\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}\textrm{ is a semimetric}\},\ B^{>}_{n}:=\{{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}:\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}\textrm{ is a metric}\},

\displaystyle R^{+}_{n}:=\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{0\}^{n-2},\ R^{++}_{n}:=\mathbb{R}_{++}\times\{0\}^{n-2},\ R^{-}_{n}:=-R^{+}_{n},\ \textnormal{and}\ R^{--}_{n}:=-R^{++}_{n}.

Notice that
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}\subseteq B^{\geq}_{n}
and
\mathbb{R}_{++}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-2}\subseteq B^{>}_{n}
(See Lemma
A.1
and Corollary
A.2
). If
\mu=\mathbf{0}
or
{\bm{\beta}}=\mathbf{0}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is the
trivial
semimetric constantly equal to
0
. The following proposition answers the questions above.

Proposition 1
.

If
n=2
, then

\displaystyle\{({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\textnormal{ is a semimetric}\}

\displaystyle=R^{++}_{2}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{2}\sqcup R^{--}_{2}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{2})\sqcup\{0\}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\{({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\textnormal{ is a metric}\}

\displaystyle=R^{++}_{2}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{>}_{2}\sqcup R^{--}_{2}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}^{>}_{2}).

If
n\geq 3
, then

\displaystyle\{({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}:{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\textnormal{ is a semimetric}\}=

\displaystyle B_{n}^{\geq}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}\cup R^{+}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}

\displaystyle\cup(-B_{n}^{\geq}){\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+})\cup R^{-}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})

\displaystyle\cup\{\mathbf{0}\}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cup\mathbb{R}^{n-1}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\{\mathbf{0}\}

and

\displaystyle\{({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}:{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\textnormal{ is a metric}\}=

\displaystyle B^{>}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{++}_{n}\cup R^{++}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{>}_{n}

\displaystyle\cup(-B^{>}_{n}){\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}^{++}_{n})\cup R^{--}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}^{>}_{n}).

The proof of this result is based on the restrictions imposed by two of the defining properties of semimetrics. In particular, apart for the trivial cases, the non-negativity of the semimetric imposes that
\mu
belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
. Furthermore, if
{\bm{\beta}}\notin\mathbb{R}_{+}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\{0\}^{n-2}
, the triangular inequality leads to the non-negativity of
\mu
, that is
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
.
Since
\mathrm{d}^{-\mu}_{-{\bm{\beta}}}={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
, it is possible to cover all the possible semimetrics by restricting to
B_{n}^{\geq}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\geq}
.

4.3.
Properties of the weighted top-difference distance

Uniqueness and neutrality

The first properties we discuss concern the uniqueness of the weights and measure of a given
\left({\bm{\beta}},\mu\right)
-top difference distance.

Proposition 2
.

For any
n\geq 3
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, we have that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}=\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\gamma}}
if and only if
{\bm{\beta}}={\bm{\gamma}}
.

Notice that, if
n=2
and
\mu([2])\neq 0
, then the claim of Proposition
2
holds.
Under stronger conditions a specular argument holds for the uniqueness of the measure.

Proposition 3
.

If
n\geq 3
, for all
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{>}_{n}\sqcup(-B^{>}_{n})
,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}=\mathrm{d}^{\nu}_{\bm{\beta}}
if and only if
\mu=\nu
.

However, it is possible to have
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}=\mathrm{d}^{\nu}_{\bm{\gamma}}
for distinct
{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}
and
\mu,\nu
. Indeed, for any
\alpha\neq 0
, if
\nu=\alpha\mu
and
{\bm{\gamma}}={\bm{\beta}}/\alpha
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}=\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\nu}
.

We now introduce an invariance property called
neutrality
. A distance function is neutral if it treats all alternatives equally meaning that it is invariant under relabeling the candidates. Formally, a function
\mathrm{d}:\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}\times\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is
neutral
if for every permutations
\sigma,\pi,\tau\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

\mathrm{d}(\tau\sigma,\tau\pi)=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi).

This means that
\mathrm{d}
is invariant under the canonical left action of
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
on itself. In particular, we have that multiplying a permutation on the left by
\tau
is equivalent to relabelling the alternatives from
(1,\ldots,n)
to
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})
.
Since the measure
\mu
in
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
captures the relative importance of the alternatives, it is natural to expect that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is neutral if and only if
\mu
gives the same importance to all options, that is when
\mu
is proportional to the counting measure. Formally,

Proposition 4
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{>}_{n}\sqcup(-B^{>}_{n})
. Then,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is neutral if and only if
\mu
is constant.

Computability

At first glance, the computation of the distance
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
between any two linear orders
\sigma
and
\pi
seems to be highly inefficient. The main reason being that it requires to compute the maximal elements according to both
\sigma
and
\pi
for
O(2^{n})
subsets. Following the methods developed by
[
46
]
, we now provide a convenient reformulation of
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
that allows to compute the distance between two permutations in polynomial time. Furthermore, as it turns out in our proofs, the formulas provided in this section will play a crucial role also beyond its computational convenience.

Fix a measure
\mu
, weights
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, and
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Notice that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|S|}\mu\left(\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\sum_{x\in S}\beta_{|S|}\mu\left(x\right)\mathbf{1}_{\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi)}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:x\in S}\beta_{|S|}\mathbf{1}_{\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi)}(x)\right)\mu\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\sum_{S:x\in S\subseteq[n],|S|=k}\mathbf{1}_{\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi)}(x)\right)\mu\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\theta_{x,k}(\sigma,\pi)\right)\mu\left(x\right)

where
\theta_{x,k}(\sigma,\pi)
is the number of all subsets
S
of cardinality
k
such that
x\in\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi)
. We now
compute

\theta_{x,k}(\sigma,\pi)
for each
x
and
k
. To this end fix
x\in[n]
and define the following

\displaystyle A_{x}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle:=x^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap x^{\downarrow,\pi},

\displaystyle B_{x}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle:=x^{\uparrow,\sigma}\cap x^{\downarrow,\pi},

\displaystyle C_{x}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle:=x^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap x^{\uparrow,\pi}

\displaystyle a_{x}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle:=|A_{x}(\sigma,\pi)|,

\displaystyle b_{x}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle:=|B_{x}(\sigma,\pi)|,

\displaystyle c_{x}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle:=|C_{x}(\sigma,\pi)|.

Let
S
be such that
|S|=k
, then
x\in M(S,\sigma)\setminus M(S,\pi)
if and only if
S=\{x\}\sqcup K\sqcup L
for some non empty
L\subseteq C_{x}(\sigma,\pi)
of cardinality
j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
and some
K\subseteq A_{x}(\sigma,\pi)
with cardinality
k-1-j
. The number of sets
S
of cardinality
k
such that
x\in M(S,\sigma)\setminus M(S,\pi)
is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\binom{c_{x}}{j}\binom{a_{x}}{k-1-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{c_{x}}{j}\binom{a_{x}}{k-1-j}-\binom{c_{x}}{0}\binom{a_{x}}{k-1}

\displaystyle=\binom{a_{x}+c_{x}}{k-1}-\binom{a_{x}}{k-1}

where the last equality follows from the Chu-Vandermonde identity.
1
1

1

We recall the Chu-Vandermonde identity:

\binom{m+\ell}{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\binom{m}{j}\binom{\ell}{r-j}.

Similarly, the number of sets
S
of cardinality
k
such that
x\in M(S,\pi)\setminus M(S,\sigma)
is

\binom{a_{x}+b_{x}}{k-1}-\binom{a_{x}}{k-1}.

Thus,

\theta_{x,k}(\sigma,\pi)=\binom{a_{x}+b_{x}}{k-1}+\binom{a_{x}+c_{x}}{k-1}-2\binom{a_{x}}{k-1}.

Now we define

f_{\bm{\beta}}:t\mapsto\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\binom{t}{k-1}.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\theta_{x,k}(\sigma,\pi)=f_{\bm{\beta}}(a_{x}(\sigma,\pi)+b_{x}(\sigma,\pi))+f_{\bm{\beta}}(a_{x}(\sigma,\pi)+c_{x}(\sigma,\pi))-2f_{\bm{\beta}}(a_{x}(\sigma,\pi)).

Next, notice that
A_{x}(\sigma,\pi)\sqcup B_{x}(\sigma,\pi)=x^{\downarrow,\pi}
; whence
a_{x}(\sigma,\pi)+b_{x}(\sigma,\pi)=|x^{\uparrow,\pi}|
.
Similarly,
a_{x}(\sigma,\pi)+c_{x}(\sigma,\pi)=|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}|
. Combining all these computations yield

(2)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\pi}|)+f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}|)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap x^{\downarrow,\pi}|)\right)\mu(x).

In particular, by using the Pascal’s identity, the above formula implies that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
can be computed in time
O(n^{2})
. Furthermore, the simplicity of the presented formulas will come in handy in the social choice application and in the algorithmic part of this work.

4.4.
Axiomatic characterization

In this section we provide an axiomatic foundation for the class of weighted top-difference distances presented above. As a pivotal step to our analysis, we first
isolate
the implications of some
betweenness
axioms, previously studied in the literature
only in conjunction
with invariance properties.

It is well known that for any two permutations
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, their Kendall distance
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)
is equal to the minimum number of adjacent transpositions that transform
\sigma
into
\pi
. Therefore, the set of all
paths
of adjacent transpositions that transform
\sigma
into
\pi
in
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)
-steps is not empty. Formally, the following is nonempty for any
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

T(\sigma,\pi):=\left\{\left(t_{a_{k},a_{k}+1}\right)_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}:\pi=\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}t_{a_{k},a_{k}+1}\right\}.

Given an element
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
and a
k\in[\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)]
, we denote by
a_{k}(\mathbf{t})
the smallest index of the
k^{\textrm{th}}
transposition. Moreover, we denote by
i_{k}(\mathbf{t})<j_{k}(\mathbf{t})
the unique elements in
[n]
that are exchanged by the
k^{\textrm{th}}
transposition.
It follows that if
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
, then,

\pi=\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}=\left(\coprod_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}t_{i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t})}\right)\sigma.

To ease the notation, when
\mathbf{t}
is clear from the context we write
a_{k},i_{k},j_{k}
omitting their dependence from
\mathbf{t}
. Before providing our axioms we need to also define the employed betweenness condition. Given distinct
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we say that
\omega
is
in between

\sigma
and
\pi
if, over all menus of size two, when
\sigma
and
\pi
agree,
\omega
agrees with them; formally,

\forall i\neq j\in\left[n\right],\,\,\,\,M(\{i,j\},\sigma)=M(\{i,j\},\pi)\implies M(\{i,j\},\omega)=M(\{i,j\},\sigma).

If this happens we write
\sigma-\omega-\pi
. The rankings
\pi^{1},\pi^{2},\ldots,\pi^{s}
are said to be
on a line
, denoted by
\pi^{1}-\pi^{2}-\cdots-\pi^{s}
, if for every
i,j
, and
k
for which
1\leq i<j<k\leq s
, we have that
\pi^{i}-\pi^{j}-\pi^{k}
.

We now introduce the axioms that we will employ in the subsequent characterizations. We provide an interpretation of all these axioms immediately before each result in which they are employed.

Axioms
.

Dio Merda

A
.1

For all
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, if
\sigma-\omega-\pi
, then
\mathrm{d}(\pi,\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\omega)+\mathrm{d}(\omega,\sigma)
.

A
.2

For all distinct
i,j,k,\ell\in[n]
,
\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{I},t_{i,j})+\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{I},t_{k,\ell})=\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{I},t_{i,k})+\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{I},t_{j,\ell})
.

A
.3

For all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
disagreeing on more than one pair of elements there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, distinct from
\sigma
and
\pi
such that
\sigma-\omega-\pi
and
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\omega)+\mathrm{d}(\omega,\pi)
.

A
.4

For all distinct
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
a\in[n-1]
,

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1}).

A
.5

For all
\sigma,\pi,\rho,\tau\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and distinct
a,b\in[n-1]
with

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})\textrm{ and }I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})=I(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})

we have that

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1}).

A
.6

For all
\sigma,\pi,\rho,\tau\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
a\in[n-1]
with

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})\sqcup I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})\sqcup I(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1}),

we have that

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1}).

Axiom
A.1
axiom requires the triangle inequality to hold as an equality for
all
permutations that lie on a line. We now completely characterize the distances satisfying this axiom.

Theorem 5
.

A semimetric
\mathrm{d}
satisfies axiom
A.1
if and only if there exists a symmetric function
g:[n]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
g(i,i)=0
for all
i\in[n]
and such that for any
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
,

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}{}g(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))=\sum_{(i,j)\in I(\sigma,\pi)}g(i,j).

Thus, Axiom
A.1
guarantees a simple expression for the distance function, only depending on the
\binom{n}{2}
numbers
g(i,j)
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
.

Remark 6
.

Some scholars considered the following stronger version of Axiom
A.1
: For all
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,
\mathrm{d}(\pi,\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\omega)+\mathrm{d}(\omega,\sigma)
if
and only if
\sigma-\omega-\pi
. Using this stronger version in Theorem
5
would require to work with a metric instead of a semimetric. The statement of the theorem would be the same apart from
\mathrm{d}
being required to be a metric and
g
to take strictly positive values outside of the diagonal.

In order to achieve additive separability of the function
g
, we apply Axiom
A.2
. The resulting semimetric turns out to be the
binary
version of
\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{{\bm{\beta}}}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{K}
, as shown below.

Theorem 7
.

A semimetric
\mathrm{d}
satisfies axioms
A.1
and
A.2
if and only if there exists
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
such that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
,

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}{}\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))=\sum_{(i,j)\in I(\sigma,\pi)}\mu(i)+\mu(j)=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:|S|=2}\mu\left(\triangle_{S}(\sigma,\pi)\right).

Therefore, in order to characterize
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
without the restriction to binary comparisons, we need to weaken Axiom
A.1
. Such weakening is shown to be provided by Axiom
A.3
which requires the triangular inequality to hold as equality only for
some
permutation between
\sigma
and
\pi
. First, it is possible see (Proposition
D.5
in the Appendix) that
A.3
is equivalent to a metric
\mathrm{d}
being a graphic distance on the permutahedron, that is the polytope with vertices the permutations in
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and as edges the couples of permutations with Kendall distance equal to one.
2
2

2

The reader can consult Figure
1
right before the Appendix.
In
[
31
]
, the authors prove that by joining Axiom
A.3
with neutrality (which they refer to as
left-invariance
), it is possible to characterize the class of weighted Kendall metrics.
3
3

3

We notice that Axiom
A.3
,
left-invariance
and
right-invariance
, together force the metric to be a multiple of the
\mathrm{d}_{K}
. Indeed all the metrics satisfying just left and right invariance must satisfy
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\mathrm{d}(\tau\sigma\tau^{-1},\tau\pi\tau^{-1})
.

Axiom
A.4
requires the length of each edge of the permutahedron to depend exclusively on the elements that are transposed and by the positions at which they occur. By combining them, we obtain

Theorem 8
.

A semimetric
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axioms
A.3
and
A.4
if and only if there exists
g:[n-1]{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}[n]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
symmetric in the last two entries with
g_{\cdot}(i,i)=0
for all
i\in[n]
such that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

(3)

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}g_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t})).

Now assuming Axioms
A.5
and
A.6
, that impose proportionality and additive separability conditions, we are able to retrieve
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
.

Theorem 9
.

For a non-trivial semimetric
\mathrm{d}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axioms
A.3
,
A.5
, and
A.6
.

(ii)

There are
{\bm{\phi}}=\left(\phi_{a}\right)_{a=1}^{n-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
and
({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in B_{n}^{\geq}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}\cup R^{n}_{+}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
such that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

(4)

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}\left(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))\right)=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|S|}\mu\left(\triangle_{S}(\sigma,\pi)\right).

Moreover,
\mu
can be chosen so that
\sum_{i}\mu(i)=1
.

In addition, given any measure
\mu
there exists a bijection
F
mapping
{\bm{\beta}}
into
{\bm{\phi}}
.

Relation between
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\phi}}

We now explore in more detail the relationship between the weights
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\phi}}
, when
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is assumed to be a non-trivial semimetric. Let
F:{\bm{\beta}}\mapsto{\bm{\phi}}
be defined as

F:{\bm{\beta}}\mapsto\left(\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\binom{n-a-1}{k-2}\right)_{a=1}^{n-1}.

As we show in the proof of Theorem
D.8
,
F
is the bijective linear map associating each
{\bm{\beta}}
to the weights
{\bm{\phi}}
as in (
20
). Recall that if
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{++}
, then for all
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{>}_{n}
,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a metric. Moreover, define
\Phi_{n}:=F(B^{>}_{n})
. It is easy to verify
4
4

4

If
\bm{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n-1}
, then for any
a\in[n-1]
and distinct
i,j\in[n]
,
(\mu(i)+\mu(j))\phi_{a}>0
and thus, by equation (
20
),
\mathrm{d}_{F^{-1}(\bm{\phi})}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)>0
for any distinct
\sigma,\tau
. Conversely, if there is some
\phi_{a}\leq 0
, then
\mathrm{d}_{F^{-1}(\bm{\phi})}^{\mu}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=(\mu(\sigma_{a})+\mu(\sigma_{a+1}))\phi_{a}\leq 0
.
that
\Phi=\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n-1}
.
An interesting subset of
B^{>}_{n}=F^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n-1})
is provided by

B_{n}^{*}:=\mathbb{R}_{++}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-2}\subseteq B^{>}_{n}.

Indeed, negative values of
\beta_{j}
would be difficult to interpret as the importance of disagreements in menus of size
j
. Moreover,
\Phi_{n}^{*}:=F(B_{n}^{*})\subseteq\Phi_{n}
corresponds to the set of positive and totally monotone sequences. In particular, if
{\bm{\beta}}\in B_{n}^{*}
, then
F({\bm{\beta}})
is non-increasing, meaning that swaps in the top positions are more significant than swaps in the bottom ones. Formally,

Proposition 10
.

\Phi_{n}^{*}=\left\{\bm{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n-1}:\forall k\in[0,n),\forall j\in[1,n-k),\ (-1)^{k}(\Delta^{k}\bm{\phi})_{j}\geq 0\right\},

where

(\Delta^{k}f)_{j}:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i+k}\binom{k}{i}f(j+i).

Example 11
.

The extremal elements of
\Phi_{n}^{*}
are the exponentials
\phi_{k}=c\alpha^{-k}
for
\alpha\geq 1
,
c>0
. In this case we have that
(if
{\bm{\beta}}=F^{-1}({\bm{\phi}})
)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}(-1)^{k+j}\binom{k-2}{j}\phi_{n-1-j}

\displaystyle=c(-1)^{k}\alpha^{1-n}\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\binom{k-2}{j}(-\alpha)^{j}

\displaystyle=c\alpha^{1-n}(\alpha-1)^{k-2}

Thus
{\bm{\beta}}
also takes an exponential form with scale
\alpha-1\geq 0
. (If
\alpha=1
we adopt the convention
0^{0}=1
and set
\beta_{2}=c
.)
∎

Some special cases

As we noticed in §
4.1
, our distance
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
relates to well-known dissimilarity functions established in the literature. In particular,

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,\ldots,1)
, then we recover the distance described in
[
46
]
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
we get the metrics proposed in
[
32
]
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}=\left((\alpha-1)^{k-2}\right)_{k=2}^{n}
for some
\alpha>1
and
\mu
is the counting measure, then we recover the
unavailable candidate model
of
[
42
]
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
and
\mu
is the counting measure, then we recover the Kendall distance proposed in
[
36
]
and axiomatized in
[
34
]
.

•

If
\mu
is the counting measure, then we recover the weighted Kendall distance proposed and axiomatized by
[
31
]
.

•

In
[
39
]
a particular case of the class of metrics characterized in Theorem
7
is considered. More specifically, the authors set
g(i,j)=w(i)w(j)
for some function
w:[n]\to\mathbb{R}_{++}
.

Isometric embedding

Isometric embedding results can be used to translate least squares methods of approximation from one space to another. In the context of finite metric space, this approach turns out to be important to avoid the difficulties inherent to the lack of convexity in optimization problems. In the working paper version of their work
[
46
]
, the authors provided a result showing the impossibility to embed the set of partial orders
5
5

5

Reflexive, transitive, and antisymmetric binary relations.
over
[n]
in any strictly convex normed linear space, whenever such set is endowed with
\mathrm{d}^{\mu}
and
n\geq 3
. Here, we strengthen their result showing that it applies to a larger family of metrics
defined on the smaller collection of linear orders. Before that we recall two definitions. We say that a metric space
(X,d_{X})
can be
isometrically embedded
in a metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
if there exists
f:X\to Y
such that
d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))=d_{X}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
. A (real) normed linear space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is
strictly convex
if for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
and distinct
x,y\in X
with
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
we have

\|\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\|<1.

Proposition 12
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
\mathrm{d}
be any metric on
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
satisfying axioms
A.3
. Then,
(\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n},\mathrm{d})
cannot be isometrically embedded in any strictly convex (real) normed linear space.

In particular, we have that for all
n\geq 3
, when
(\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n},{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}})
is a metric space, it cannot be isometrically embedded in any strictly convex (real) normed linear space.

5.
Social choice theory

We study the properties of the median preference function induced by the class of distances introduced in the previous sections. To this end we start providing a primer on the terminology used in Social Choice theory.

Notation and terminology

Throughout the section we fix a nonempty finite set
C=[n]
of candidates with
n\geq 2
, over which decision makers (or
voters
) express their preferences. Each voter is represented by a ranking
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. A profile of voters
V=(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m})
(also called,
electorate
) is a finite ordered list of rankings over
[n]
.
6
6

6

With abuse of notation we denote by
|V|
the dimension of
V
.
We define by

\mathbf{V}_{n}:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}^{k}

the set of all possible lists of voters over
C
. Often, we may write

\left(s_{1}v^{1},s_{2}v^{2},\ldots,s_{m}v^{m}\right)

to identify a profile of voters where for each
j=1,\ldots,m
, the share of voters whose preferences are expressed by
v^{j}
is
s_{j}\in\left[0,1\right]
.
7
7

7

Sometimes we replace
s_{j}
with
n_{j}
, where the latter represents the number of voters in the electorate with preference
\sigma^{j}
.
Pivotal to our analysis are
preference correspondences
. In particular, a preference correspondence
P:{\mathbf{V}_{n}}\rightrightarrows\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
associates to each profile of voters a subset of
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Given a preference correspondence
P
and a profile of voters
V
we sometimes refer to
P(V)
as the set of
consensus preferences
. Each preference correspondence
P
induces a
social choice correspondence

W_{P}:{\mathbf{V}_{n}}\rightrightarrows C
defined as

W_{P}(V):=\bigcup\limits_{v\in P(V)}M\left(C,v\right),

namely the set of
winners
identified by all the consensus preferences.

An active part of the literature on Social Choice theory focuses on
distance-based voting methods
. Specifically, a semimetric
\mathrm{d}:\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
induces a
median preference correspondence
as follows:

P_{\mathrm{d}}:V\mapsto\argmin\limits_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{|V|}\mathrm{d}(\sigma,v^{j}).

In the rest of this section, we focus on the median preference correspondence induced by
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
. To ease notation we set
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}:=P_{{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}}
and
P_{\bm{\beta}}:=P_{\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}}
and we define
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
and
W_{\bm{\beta}}
analogously. Given
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we also conveniently denote the cumulative distance by

(5)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V):=\sum_{j=1}^{|V|}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,v^{j}).

When we refer to the Kemeny-Young preference correspondence, we mean
P_{\mathrm{d}_{K}}
.

5.1.
Social choice properties

We proceed to study the main properties of the preference and social choice correspondences
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
and
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
. In doing so we briefly recall the definitions of such properties. Fix a preference correspondence
P
. We say that
P
satisfies
anonymity
if for all
(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m})\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and permutations
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{m}
we have

P(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m})=P(v^{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,v^{\sigma_{m}}).

Anonymity requires that voters’ labels do not matter when determining the set of consensus preferences. A specular requirement is
neutrality
. In particular,
P
is
neutral
if for all
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and permutations
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
we have
8
8

8

By
\sigma(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m})=(\sigma v^{1},\ldots,\sigma v^{m})
, similarly,
\sigma\{w^{1},\ldots,w^{k}\}=\{\sigma w^{1},\ldots,\sigma w^{k}\}
.

P(\sigma V)=\sigma P(V).

While anonymity is a property that immediately holds for all median preference correspondences, neutrality is not always satisfied.
It is known in the literature that
P_{\mathrm{d}}
is neutral whenever
\mathrm{d}
is neutral (see Lemma 2 and its proof in
[
17
]
). As it concerns our metric, we have that if
\beta_{2}>0
and
\mu
is proportional to the counting measure, then
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
is neutral. On the other hand, if
\mu
is not proportional to the counting measure, then
P_{\mathrm{d}}^{\mu}
might not be neutral, as shown in the next example.

Example 13
.

Let
n=3
,
V=((1,2,3),(3,1,2),(2,3,1))
,
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,0)
and,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{++}_{3}
be such that
\mu_{3}>\max\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}
. Now, if
\sigma=(3,2,1)
, then
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)=\{(2,3,1)\}
and
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma V)=\{(1,3,2)\}\neq\{(2,1,3)\}=\sigma P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
showing that
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
is not neutral.
9
9

9

The relative computations are reported in the Appendix.

∎

However, we found that a metric
\mathrm{d}
satisfying Axioms
A.3
and
A.4
is neutral if and only if the corresponding
P_{\mathrm{d}}^{f}
is neutral for any increasing and strictly convex function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
10
10

10

We are defining

P^{f}_{\mathrm{d}}:V\mapsto\argmin_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{|V|}f(\mathrm{d}(\sigma,v^{i})).

A powerful property that stands at that basis of the characterizations of the Kemeny-Young method (
[
56
]
) and positional voting rules (
[
54
]
) is
reinforcing
.
11
11

11

Some authors also refer to such property as
consistency
.
In words, reinforcing requires that whenever two separate committees share the same consensus preferences, then deliberating together their consensus preferences should not change. Formally, a preference correspondence satisfies
reinforcing
if

(6)

P(V_{1})\cap P(V_{2})\neq\emptyset\Longrightarrow P(V_{1}\oplus V_{2})=P(V_{1})\cap P(V_{2}),

where
\oplus
is the concatenation operator.
12
12

12

More explicitly,

(v^{1},\ldots,v^{k})\oplus(w^{1},\ldots,w^{k}):=(v^{1},\ldots,v^{k},w^{1},\ldots,w^{k}).

As it is relevant for our analysis we distinguish between reinforcing for
P
and
W_{P}
. The latter is defined by swapping
P
with
W_{P}
in (
6
), and we refer to it as
reinforcing for winners
.

Proposition 14
.

If
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric, then,
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
satisfies reinforcing.

Proposition 15
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
be a semimetric. If
{\beta}_{k}=0
for all
2\leq k\leq n-1
, then
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
satisfies reinforcing for winners.
Moreover, if
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
and
\mu
is proportional to the counting measure, then
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
satisfies reinforcing winners if and only if
{\beta}_{k}=0
for all
2\leq k\leq n-1
.

An other important requirement is
monotonicity
. Monotonicity of a preference correspondence requires that upranking a winning candidate cannot harm him. Formally, for a profile of voters
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and
c\in C
, we denote by
V^{c}
an
upranking
. More specifically,
V^{c}
is obtained by upranking
c
for some voters, coeteris paribus.
13
13

13

An upranking of candidate
k
for
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
is a ranking
\pi
such that for all
i,j\in[n]\setminus\{k\}
we have that
i>_{\sigma}j
iff
i>_{\pi}j
and there is some
i\neq k
such that
i>_{\sigma}k
and
i<_{\pi}k
.

We say that
P
is
monotone
, whenever

c\in W_{P}(V)\Longrightarrow\forall V^{c},\;c\in W_{P}(V^{c}).

Proposition 16
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
. Then,
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
is monotone.

Arguably the most iconic aspects of voting rules are related to
majority
. For a given profile of voters
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and
c\in C
, we denote by
n(V,c)
the number of voters that consider
c
as their most favourite alternative
minus
the number of voters who do not. A preference correspondence
P
satisfies the
majority principle
if for all
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and
c\in C
,

n(V,c)\geq 0\Longrightarrow c\in W_{P}(V).

Proposition 17
.

If
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric, then
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
satisfies the majority principle.

The majority principle readily implies that
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
also satisfies unanimity. We prove that actually more holds true. In particular, we provide a
blockwise
version of Pareto dominance. To this end we need some further notation. For all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
i\leq k\leq n
, we define

\sigma(\left[i,k\right]):=\left\{\sigma_{i},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\right\},

the
set
of candidates in positions from
i
to
k
according to
\sigma
.
A preference correspondence
P
satisfies

•

blockwise Pareto winners
if for all
V=\left(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m}\right)\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,

\forall k\in[n],\;\left(\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\left[m\right],v^{j}(\left[k\right])=v^{j^{\prime}}(\left[k\right])\Rightarrow\forall v\in P(V),\ v(\left[k\right])=v^{1}(\left[k\right])\right)

•

blockwise Pareto losers
if for all
V=\left(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m}\right)\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,

\forall k\in[n],\;\left(\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\left[m\right],v^{j}(\left[k\right]^{\mathsf{c}})=v^{j^{\prime}}(\left[k\right]^{\mathsf{c}})\Rightarrow\forall v\in P(V),\ v(\left[k\right]^{\mathsf{c}})=v^{1}(\left[k\right]^{\mathsf{c}})\right)

Proposition 18
.

For all
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
with
\beta_{2}>0
,
P_{\bm{\beta}}
satisfies blockwise Pareto winners and losers.

At this point one may ask whether
P_{{\bm{\beta}}}
satisfies an even stronger version of the blockwise Pareto property. That is, whether for all
(v^{j})_{j=1}^{m}\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}

\forall k,i\in[n],\;\left(\forall j,j^{\prime}\in\left[m\right],v^{j}(\left[k,i\right])=v^{j^{\prime}}(\left[k,i\right])\Rightarrow\forall v\in P(V),\ v(\left[k,i\right])=v^{1}(\left[k,i\right])\right)

This does not hold in general as shown in the following example.

Example 19
.

C=\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
and
V=\left((1,2,3,4),(3,2,1,4),(4,2,3,1)\right)
. Clearly,
2
is in the second position for all voters, though it can be shown that for
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
,

P_{\bm{\beta}}(V)=\{(2,3,1,4)\}.

∎

A further condition is a partitionwise version of the Pareto property. In particular, we say that a preference correspondence
P
has the
partitionwise Pareto property
if for any increasing sequence
(k_{i})_{i=0}^{\ell}
with
k_{0}=0
and
k_{\ell}=n
, and any profile of voters
V=(v^{j})_{j=1}^{m}
, it holds

\displaystyle\forall i\in[\ell],\forall j,j^{\prime}\in[m],\ v^{j}((k_{i-1},k_{i}])=v^{j^{\prime}}((k_{i-1},k_{i}])

\displaystyle\Downarrow

\displaystyle\forall i\in[\ell],\forall v\in P(V),\ v((k_{i-1},k_{i}])=v^{1}((k_{i-1},k_{i}]).

Corollary 20
.

For all
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
with
\beta_{2}>0
,
P_{\bm{\beta}}
satisfies the partitionwise Pareto property.

A stronger majority condition is the
Condorcet principle.
For all
i,j\in[n]
and
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
, we denote by
n_{i,j}(V)
the number of voters who prefer
i
to
j

minus
the number of voters who do not. A preference correspondence
P
satisfies the
Condorcet principle
if

n_{i,j}(V)>0\Longrightarrow\not\exists\sigma\in P(V):(j,i)\in\mathrm{adj}(\sigma)

(7)

and

n_{i,j}(V)=0\Longrightarrow\left(\left(\exists\sigma\in P(V):(i,j)\in\mathrm{adj}(\sigma)\right)\Longleftrightarrow\left(\exists\pi\in P(V):(j,i)\in\mathrm{adj}(\pi)\right)\right),

where by adj
(\sigma)
we denote the set of all couples
(i,j)
adjacent in
\sigma
and with
i>_{\sigma}j
.
Notice that a much stronger requirement in the style of the Condorcet principle would be the following,

(8)

n_{i,k}(V)>0\Longrightarrow\forall\sigma\in P(V),\ i>_{\sigma}k.

The distinction between (
7
) and (
8
) lies on the adjacency between
i
and
k
which must be satisfied for the Condorcet principle to hold, but it is not required by (
8
). Even though also this latter and stronger requirement appears to be meaningful, it is not satisfied even by the Kemeny-Young’s rule as shown in the upcoming example.

Example 21
.

Let
C=\left\{1,2,3\right\}
and
V=((1,2,3),(2,3,1),(3,1,2))
.
Suppose that
\mu
is the counting measure and that
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,0)
. Then,
n_{1,2}(V)=1,\ n_{3,1}(V)=1,\ n_{2,3}(V)=1
.
Notwithstanding, we have that

P_{\bm{\beta}}(V)=\left\{(1,2,3),(2,3,1),(3,1,2)\right\}

and thus
P_{\bm{\beta}}
does not satisfy condition (
8
).
∎

Given a profile of voters
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
we say that a candidate
i
is
Condorcet for

V
, if
i
is preferred to all other candidates in binary comparisons by a majority of voters, i.e.,
n_{i,j}(V)\geq 0
, for all
j\neq i
. The set of Condorcet candidates for a profile
V
, will be denoted by
\mathcal{C}(V)
. A social choice correspondence
W
satisfies the
Condorcet principle
if
\mathcal{C}(V)\subseteq W(V)
for all
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
. The following result provides a full characterization of the Condorcet principles for both
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
and
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}
.

Proposition 22
.

Suppose
n\geq 3
,
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
with
\beta_{2}>0
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{++}_{n}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

W_{\bm{\beta}}^{\mu}
satisfies the Condorcet principle.

(ii)

P_{\bm{\beta}}^{\mu}
satisfies the Condorcet principle.

(iii)

\beta_{k}=0
for all
k\geq 3
.

Such characterization strenghtens the results of
[
56
]
highlighting the restrictions to binary comparisons imposed by the Condorcet principle.

6.
Computational Aspects

In this section we investigate the computational aspects of the preference aggregation problem. In §
6.1
, we advance an integer program formulation to compute a consensus preference exactly. Given the impossibility of this approach to scale efficiently with the number of alternatives (
n
) and voters (
m
), we then turn our attention to approximation algorithms. Specifically, in §
6.2
, we provide a set of generalized Diaconis-Graham inequalities and a unified framework to analyze them. These allow to obtain approximation algorithms for the corresponding preference aggregation problem. Finally, in §
6.3
, we propose some truncation ideas building upon in
[
42
]
. By leveraging them, we retrieve a Polynomial-Time Approximation Scheme (PTAS) for the rank aggregation problem.

6.1.
Integer program formulation

We recall that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)
is computable in polynomial time via the following identity

(9)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{x\in[n]}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}|)+f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\pi}|)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap x^{\downarrow,\pi}|)\right)\mu(x).

Observe that any permutation
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
can be associated to a binary square matrix
P^{\sigma}\in\{0,1\}^{n{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}n}
such that, for all
i\neq j
,
P^{\sigma}_{i,j}=1
if and only if
i>_{\sigma}j
. Conversely any
P\in\{0,1\}^{n{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}n}
which satisfies the following properties

(C.1)

\displaystyle P_{i,i}=0,\;\textnormal{for all $i$}\in[n]\;

\displaystyle P_{i,j}+P_{j,i}=1,\;\textnormal{ for all $i$}\neq j\in[n]\;\textnormal{(completeness)}

\displaystyle P_{i,j}+P_{j,k}-1\leq P_{i,k},\;\textnormal{ for all $i,j,k\in[n]$ distinct}\;\textnormal{ (transitivity)},

determines a unique linear order. Therefore,
\sigma\mapsto P^{\sigma}
is a well-defined bijection. Given this formulation, we can see that
|{i}^{\downarrow,\sigma}|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}P^{\sigma}_{i,j}
and
|{i}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{i}^{\downarrow,\pi}|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}P^{\sigma}_{i,j}P^{\pi}_{i,j}
.

Consider a profile of
m
voters
V\in\mathbf{V}_{n}
and their corresponding matrices
(P^{{v}})_{v=1}^{m}
. By using (
9
) and the matrix representation, we can obtain the following function that is equivalent (for optimization purposes) to the objective function of the preference aggregation problem.
14
14

14

Observe that the middle term depends only of
P^{v}
and not on
P
; therefore, we can neglect it when computing an aggregate ranking.

(10)

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}P^{{v}}_{i,j}\right)\right)\mu({i})=

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(r+s)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}(s)\right)\mu(i)\mathbf{1}\begin{bmatrix}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(1-P_{i,j}^{v})P_{i,j}=r\\
\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}^{v}P_{i,j}=s\end{bmatrix}.

We linearize the this function by introducing binary variables
Q^{v,i}_{r,s}
satisfying the following constraints

(C.2)

\displaystyle Q^{v,i}_{r,s}\leq 1+\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}(1-P_{i,j}^{{v}})-r\right)\;\textnormal{and}\;Q^{v,i}_{r,s}\leq 1+\frac{1}{n}\left(r-\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}(1-P_{i,j}^{v})\right)

\displaystyle Q^{v,i}_{r,s}\leq 1+\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}P_{i,j}^{v}-s\right),\;Q^{v,i}_{r,s}\leq 1+\frac{1}{n}\left(s-\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}P_{i,j}^{{v}}\right),\;\textnormal{and}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{n}Q^{v,i}_{r,s}=1.

The resulting optimization problem turns out to be

(11)

\displaystyle\min_{P,Q}

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(r+s)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}(s)\right)\mu(i)Q^{v,i}_{r,s}

\displaystyle\textrm{s.t.}

\displaystyle P\in\{0,1\}^{n{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}n},\;\forall v\in[m],\forall i\in[n],Q^{v,i}\in\{0,1\}^{n{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}n},\;\textnormal{\ref{Set of constraints 1}},\;\textnormal{and}\;\textnormal{\ref{Set of constraints 2}}.

Notably, this linear integer program is completely specified by
O(mn^{3})
variables and constraints.

6.2.
Generalized Diaconis-Graham inequalities

Here, we provide a set of generalized Diaconis-Graham inequalities and a unified framework to study them. This allows us to retrieve approximation algorithms for the corresponding rank aggregation problems (see also
[
27
]
). The celebrated result by
[
26
]
states that the Kendall and Spearman’s footrule distances are within a factor of two.
15
15

15

That is
\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}\leq\mathrm{d}_{K}\leq 2\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}
, where
\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}
denotes the Spearman’s footrule distance.
We generalize this result to the family of
({\bm{\beta}},\mu)
-top difference distances. We first focus on the
neutral
case and then we argue how to extend our results when neutrality fails.

For all
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
, the
{\bm{\beta}}
-
Spearman’s footrule distance
is defined as

\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}:(\sigma,\pi)\mapsto\sum_{x\in[n]}\big|f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}|)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\pi}|)\big|.

Notably, differently from (
2
), to compute
\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)
we do not need to evaluate all the intersections
x^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap x^{\downarrow,\pi}
. This makes the computation of the
{\bm{\beta}}
-Spearman’s footrule distance relatively easier than computing our distance.

The main result of this subsection is the following theorem which provides a unified analysis of generalized Diaconis-Graham inequalities.

Theorem 23
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
with
\beta_{2}>0
. Then, for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\gamma_{\bm{\beta}}\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi),

where
\gamma_{\bm{\beta}}:=\max_{h\in[n]}\left(1+\frac{f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-h)}{f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-(h-1))}\right).

Theorem
23
implies that for each choice of the weights
{\bm{\beta}}
, the
{\bm{\beta}}
-top difference metric and
{\bm{\beta}}
-Spearman’s footrule distance remain within a factor of two. Moreover, Theorem
23
can be applied to specific weights in order to obtain tighter bounds. In particular,

Corollary 24
.

For all permutations
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, it holds

•

For
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
,

\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq 2\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)
,

•

For
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
,
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)
,

•

For
\beta_{k}=p^{n-k}(1-p)^{k}
with
p\in(0,1)
,
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq(1+p)\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)

•

For
\beta_{k}=k
,
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\textnormal{apx}}(\sigma,\pi)
.

In their seminal work,
[
27
]
proposed to obtain polynomial-time approximation algorithms for the Kemeny rank aggregation problem by minimizing the sum of Spearman’s footrule distances via a minimum cost perfect matching algorithm.

Following a similar spirit, we leverage on Theorem
23
to provide approximation algorithms for our rank aggregation problems. First, we show that, by minimizing the sum of
{\bm{\beta}}
-Spearman’s footrule distances, we obtain
\gamma_{\bm{\beta}}
-approximations for the corresponding rank aggregation problem. Using the notation introduced in §
5
, we let

P^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}(V):=\underset{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\textnormal{argmin}}\;\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V),

for all
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

Corollary 25
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
with
\beta_{2}>0
,
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
, and
\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}}\in P^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
.
Then,

\min_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}},V)\leq\gamma_{\bm{\beta}}\min_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V).

The Hungarian algorithm can be implemented to retrieve a permutation minimizing
\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}(\cdot,V)
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
,
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
, and
\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}}\in P^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
. Then,
\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}}
is computable in
O(mn^{3})
.

By combining Theorem
23
with Corollary
25
and Lemma
26
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 27
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
with
\beta_{2}>0
and
V\in\mathbf{V}_{n}
. Then, there exists a
\gamma_{\bm{\beta}}
-approximation algorithm for the
{\bm{\beta}}
-rank aggregation problem running in time
O(mn^{3})
.

Finally, we observe that the machinery developed above can accommodate some amount of non-neutrality, as captured by the
range
of
\mu
.

Corollary 28
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
with
\beta_{2}>0
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{++}
with
u\leq\mu_{i}\leq U
, for some positive
u
and
U
. Then, for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, it holds

\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\mu,{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\gamma_{\bm{\beta}}\frac{U}{u}\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\mu,{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi),

where
\mathrm{d}^{\textnormal{apx}}_{\mu,{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi):=\sum_{x\in[n]}\big|f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\sigma}|)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(|x^{\downarrow,\pi}|)\big|\mu(x)
.

We address the question of whether it is possible to obtain competitive ratios that do not depend on the support of
\mu
in the following section.

6.3.
Polynomial-Time-Approximation schemes

In this final subsection, by building upon
[
42
]
, we provide a PTAS for the
({\bm{\beta}},\mu)
-rank aggregation problem (Algorithm
1
). We defer the reader to the discussion paragraph below Theorem
29
for a comparison with
[
42
]
.

Recall that our class of metrics penalize more mismatches happening over the most favorite alternatives compared to disagreements over least favorite ones. The intuition behind the Algorithm
1
is simple yet effective: as disagreements over least favorite alternatives are less important, we can “truncate” our metrics to account only for mismatches over the top positions and minimize the sum of these truncated “distances”. We informally refer to the corresponding minimization problem as truncated rank aggregation problem. Alternatives in the remaining positions are arbitrarily ordered. By appropriately choosing the truncation depth, we can ensure simultaneously a polynomial running time for the algorithm and a small error due to the arbitrary ordering for the least preferred alternatives.
We now introduce the truncation that we are going to exploit subsequently. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
,
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
, and
b,t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1\leq b\leq t\leq n
. The
(\mu,{\bm{\beta}},b,t)
-
top-difference truncation
is defined as

(12)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\cdot,\cdot;b,t):(\sigma,\pi)\mapsto\sum_{i=b}^{t}\left[f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)\left(\mu({\sigma_{i}})+\mu({\pi_{i}})\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}(|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\pi}|)\mu({\sigma_{i}})\right].

Notice that, for all permutations
\sigma,\pi,\omega,\tau\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
agreeing on positions
b\leq i\leq t
, we have that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi;b,t)={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\tau;b,t)
. For simplicity we denote
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V;b,t):=\sum_{j=1}^{|V|}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,v^{j};b,t)
, for all
V=(v^{j})\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
. It is observed that
\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\cdot,\cdot;1,n)=\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Given the above notation, Algorithm
1
works as follows

Input:

K\geq 1
,
V=(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m})
,
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

Set
Q\leftarrow[n]

Set
i\leftarrow 1

while

\exists c\in Q:\left|\left\{j\in[m]\mid\forall a\in Q\setminus\{c\},\ c>_{v^{j}}a\right\}\right|>\frac{m}{2}

do

{\sigma_{i}}\leftarrow c

Q\leftarrow Q\setminus\{c\}

i\leftarrow i+1

end while

K\leftarrow\min\{K,|Q|\}

\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}}\in\argmin_{\omega\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,V;i,i+K-1)
s.t.
\omega_{k}=\sigma_{k}
, for
k<i
.

Output :

\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}}

Algorithm 1

MyopicTop
[
42
]

Algorithm
1
first searches for majority candidates to be set in the top positions of the consensus ranking. Then, it solves the truncated rank aggregation problem. Before passing to the main theorem of this section we need some notation. We define

\bm{\mathcal{M}}:=\left\{\left(\mu^{(k)}\right)_{k=2}^{\infty}\in\bigtimes_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{k}:0<\inf_{k,i}\mu^{(k)}_{i}\leq\sup_{k,i}\mu^{(k)}_{i}<\infty\right\}.

The next result provides a polynomial time approximation scheme for the rank aggregation problem.

Theorem 29
.

Let
(\beta_{k})_{k=2}^{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
with
\beta_{2}>0
and satisfying

(13)

\lim_{K\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq K\vee 2}\frac{\sum_{j=2}^{t-K}\beta_{j}\binom{t-K}{j}}{\sum_{j=0}^{t-2}\beta_{j+2}\binom{t-2}{j}}=0.

Let
(\mu^{(k)})_{k=2}^{\infty}\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}
, and suppose that
0<u\leq\mu^{(k)}_{i}\leq U<\infty
, for all
i
and
k
. Consider the function

g:\frac{1}{\epsilon}\longmapsto\min\left\{K\in\mathbb{N}\bigg|\sup_{t\geq K\vee 2}\frac{\sum_{j=2}^{t-K}\beta_{j}\binom{t-K}{j}}{\sum_{j=0}^{t-2}\beta_{j+2}\binom{t-2}{j}}\leq\epsilon\right\}.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Then, for all
n\geq 2
and
V\in\mathbf{V}_{n}
,

\mathrm{d}^{\mu^{(n)}}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n}}(\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}},V)\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{d}^{\mu^{(n)}}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n}}(\sigma^{*},V),

where
\sigma^{*}\in P^{\mu^{(n)}}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n}}(V)
, and
\sigma^{\textnormal{apx}}
is the ranking obtained by running Algorithm
1
with input
K=g(\frac{12U}{u\epsilon})
,
V
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n}
and
\mu^{(n)}
. Furthermore, Algorithm
1
runs in time
O\left(n^{g\left(\frac{12U}{u\epsilon}\right)+1}mg\left(\frac{12U}{u\epsilon}\right)\right)
.

We remark that the non-polynomial dependence on
\epsilon
suffices for a PTAS, as
\epsilon
is a fixed but arbitrary constant. The following are simple applications of Theorem
29
:
16
16

16

The computations are reported in Appendix
G
.

•

If
\beta_{j}=(\alpha-1)^{j}
for
\alpha>1
, then
g(1/\epsilon)=\left\lceil\log_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(\alpha-1)^{2}\epsilon}\right)\right\rceil
.

•

If
\beta_{j}=j+1
, then
g(1/\epsilon)=\lceil\log_{2}(4/\epsilon)\rceil
.

•

If
\beta_{j}=1+(-1)^{j}
, then
g(1/\epsilon)=\lceil\log_{2}(4/\epsilon)\rceil
.

Discussion on PTAS

The
unavailable candidate model
proposed by
[
42
]
is a particular case of the distances considered in this work; it corresponds to the choice of exponential weights and counting measure. Our analysis extends their result along two dimensions: on the one hand, Theorem
29
applies to all metrics satisfying Condition (
13
) and not just exponential weights. This allows us to identify sufficient conditions on the growth rate of the weights
{\bm{\beta}}
for this approach to work. See the list above for several examples. Further, by using the analogy established in Theorem
9
, Algorithm
1
turns out to be also a PTAS for the family of weighted Kendall distances.

On the other hand, our analysis applies to the case where neutrality fails. Specifically, we show that it is possible to obtain competitive ratios that do not degrade with the support of
\mu
at the cost of increasing the running time of the algorithm. Finally, we stress out that the formulation of the weighted top-difference truncation and its applicability heavily rely on the identity (
2
).

7.
Future research

In this paper we studied a class of distances focusing on its axiomatic foundations, social choice implications, and computational features. We deem that there is much room for future research.

On the social choice aspects, it would be interesting to understand how to characterize the median voting rule induced by our distance. That is, to provide conditions for a preference correspondence
P
which hold if and only if
P=P_{{\bm{\beta}}}^{\mu}
for some
{\bm{\beta}}
and
\mu
. In addition, there are many properties of preference correspondences that we did not investigate. Strategy-proofness would be a very interesting property to study, as well as the distance rationalization aspects linked to the weighted top-difference distance.

From a more computational standpoint, it remains an open problem to show the existence of practical approximation algorithms whose competitive ratio does not degrade with the support of
\mu
. Even though we did not pursue this avenue, it is an interesting direction to study the complexity of strategic manipulation (e.g.,
[
6
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
16
]
), as well as elicitation of voters’ preferences (e.g.,
[
18
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
7
]
) under the distances proposed in this work.

Appendix

A.
Toolkit lemmas

Lemma A.1
.

For all
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}
, we have that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric.

Proof.

Non-negativity and symmetry are straightforward. Moreover, if
\sigma=\pi
, then for all
S\subseteq[n]
, we have that
M(S,\sigma)=M(S,\pi)
thus
\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma,\pi)=\emptyset
. Hence,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=0
. In conclusion, we need to show the triangle inequality. For all
S\subseteq[n]
and
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

M(S,\sigma)\bigtriangleup M(S,\pi)\subseteq(M(S,\sigma)\bigtriangleup M(S,\omega))\sqcup(M(S,\omega)\bigtriangleup M(S,\pi)).

Thus, we conclude that

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)
.∎

Corollary A.2
.

For all
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
with
\beta_{2}>0
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{++}
, we have that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a metric.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.1
, it is sufficient to prove that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=0
implies
\sigma=\pi
. Indeed, if
\sigma\neq\pi
, then we can find
i,j\in[n]
distinct so that
M(\{i,j\},\sigma)\neq M(\{i,j\},\pi)
, then

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)\geq\beta_{2}\mu(\bigtriangleup_{\{i,j\}}(\sigma,\pi))\geq\beta_{2}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j})>0.

∎

Lemma A.3
.

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
and
\beta_{2}>0
, then
f_{\bm{\beta}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is increasing and non-negative. If in addition
\beta_{2}>0
, then
f_{\bm{\beta}}
is strictly increasing and positive.

Proof.

We have that

f_{\bm{\beta}}(t)\geq\beta_{2}\binom{t}{1}=t\beta_{2}\geq 0
for all
t\geq 1
. As for the monotonicity, we have that

f_{\bm{\beta}}(t+1)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(t)=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\left(\binom{t+1}{k-1}-\binom{t}{k-1}\right)=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\binom{t}{k-2}\geq\beta_{2}\binom{t}{0}=\beta_{2}\geq 0

for all
t\geq 1
. Thus,
f_{\bm{\beta}}
is increasing. If
\beta_{2}>0
, then the previous inequalities yields the second part of the claim.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
, then
f_{\bm{\beta}}
has increasing increments. If in addition
\beta_{2}>0
, then
f_{\bm{\beta}}
has strictly increasing increments.

Proof.

Let
l\geq m
and
h\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle(f_{{\bm{\beta}}}(l+h)-f_{{\bm{\beta}}}(l))-(f_{{\bm{\beta}}}(m+h)-f_{{\bm{\beta}}}(m))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{j+1}\left(\binom{l+h}{j}-\binom{l}{j}-\binom{m+h}{j}+\binom{m}{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{j+1}\left(\sum_{k=l}^{l+h-1}\binom{k}{j-1}-\sum_{k=m}^{m+h-1}\binom{k}{j-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{j+1}\left(\sum_{k=m}^{m+h-1}\binom{k+l-m}{j-1}-\binom{k}{j-1}\right)\geq 0.

Therefore,
f_{{\bm{\beta}}}
has non-decreasing increments. If
\beta_{2}>0
, then the second part of the claim follows from analogous steps.
∎

The following three lemmas are used in the proof of Theorem
29
. First, by exploiting the fact that
\mu
is a bounded measure, we directly get the following upper bound.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
, with
\mu\leq U
,
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
and
b,t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1\leq b\leq t\leq n
. Then, for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
V\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,

(14)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V;b,t)\leq 2|V|U\sum_{i=b}^{t}f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i).

Proof.

It follows immediately from the definition.
∎

Notice that when
\mu\equiv U
,
V=(\pi,\pi,\ldots,\pi)
for some
\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and
\sigma
is
antipodal
to
\pi
, then (
14
) holds as an equality. Secondly, we provide a lower bound.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{++}
, with
\mu_{i}\geq u>0
,
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
,
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and
V=(v^{j})_{j=1}^{|V|}\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
. Suppose there exists
k\in\{0,\ldots,n-2\}
such that

|\left\{j\in[|V|]\mid{\sigma_{k+1}}>_{v^{j}}a,\;\textnormal{for all }a\in C\setminus\{{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,{\sigma_{k+1}}\}\right\}|\leq\frac{|V|}{2}.

Then,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V)\geq\frac{u|V|}{2}\phi_{k+1},
where we recall that
\phi_{j}:=f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-j)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-j-1)
.

Proof.

First of all, observe that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V)\geq u\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)
. Then, consider the following profile
\tilde{V}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor|V|/2\rfloor\}
,
\omega^{j}:=\sigma
, while, for
j\in\{\lfloor|V|/2\rfloor+1,\ldots,|V|\}
,

\omega^{j}=\sigma t_{k+1,k+2}
. By construction,
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)\geq\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\tilde{V})
and
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\tilde{V})\geq\frac{u|V|}{2}\phi_{k+1}
.
∎

Suppose we have a consensus ranking
\sigma
and remove the first
k
alternatives.
The following lemma ensures that for any consensus ranking
\pi
on
C\setminus\sigma([k])
, the concatenation

(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n-k})

is a consensus ranking on
C
. For each
A\subset[n]
, we denote by
\sigma^{-A}
the linear order we obtain by removing all candidates in
A
; for example, suppose
\sigma=(1,\ldots,{n})
and
A=\{n\}
, then
\sigma^{-A}=(1,\ldots,{n-1})
.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}
,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{++}
,
V=(v^{j})_{j=1}^{m}\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,
