\sigma\in P^{\mu}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(V)
, and
1\leq k\leq n-1
. Define
C_{1:k}:=\{{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,{\sigma_{k}}\}
. Let
\pi:\{k+1,\ldots,n\}\to C_{k+1:n}
be a bijective map minimizing

(15)

\omega\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\left(f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\omega}\right|\right)+f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}(|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\tilde{v}^{j}}|)-2f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}(|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\omega}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\tilde{v}^{j}}|)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}}),

where, for each
j\in[m]
,
\tilde{v}^{j}=(v^{j})^{-C_{1:k}}
. Then,
\sigma^{*}:=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k},\pi_{k+1},\ldots,\pi_{n})\in P^{\mu}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(V)
.

Proof.

The proof follows by observing that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{i}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\mu(i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{i}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{i}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{i}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\left(f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\tilde{v}^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\left(f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-2_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\pi}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\tilde{v}^{j}}\right|\right)\right)\mu({\sigma_{i}})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma^{*},V)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{i}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}\right|\right)\mu(i),

where (
a
) holds because, for
i\in[k+1,n]
, we have that
\sigma_{j}\notin{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}
, for all
j\leq k
. Therefore, for all
i\in[k+1,n]
, we can replace
v^{j}
with
\tilde{v}^{j}
, as
{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,v^{j}}={\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\tilde{v}^{j}}
. Furthermore, we have
f_{{\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)=f_{{\bm{\beta}}}\left(\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\right)
, as
\left|{\sigma_{i}}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right|\leq n-k-1
;
(b)
holds because
\pi
is a minimizer of (
15
) and that the central term of (
15
) does not depend on
\pi
; (
c
) holds because
\sigma^{*}
agrees with
\sigma
on the first
k
positions and with
\pi
in the remaining
n-k
, and with the same arguments underlying (
a
).
∎

Remark A.8
.

Observe that minimizing (
15
) is equivalent (up to appropriate relabeling) to minimize the cumulative sum of some (appropriately defined) top-difference distances. Specifically, let
A\subset[n]
, and define
V^{-A}:=((v^{j})^{-A})_{j=1}^{|V|}
. Then, let

•

\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}:={\bm{\beta}}_{2:n-k}

•

z:C_{k+1:n}\to[n-k]
any bijective map to relabel the indices

•

\hat{\mu}_{z(i)}:=\mu_{i}
, for all
i\in C_{k+1:n}

•

\hat{V}:=\{v\in\mathbb{S}_{n-(k+1)}\mid\exists\tilde{v}\in V^{-C_{1:k}}\;\textnormal{s.t.}\;v_{z(i)}=\tilde{v}_{i},\;\textnormal{for all }i\in C_{k+1:n}\}

Then, minimizing (
15
) is clearly equivalent to

\min_{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n-k}}\mathrm{d}^{\hat{\mu}}_{\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}}(\sigma,\hat{V}).

B.
Proofs in §
4.2

Lemma B.1
.

If
n>2
, then
\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\cap-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}=\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\cap-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
. For all distinct
i,j,k\in[n]
,

\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}=0,\ \mu_{i}+\mu_{k}=0,\ \textnormal{and}\,\mu_{j}+\mu_{k}=0

which yields
\mu_{i}=-\mu_{j}=\mu_{k}=-\mu_{i}
which implies
\mu_{i}=0
, and hence
\mu\equiv 0
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose
n>2
.
Given
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
,

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in(B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}\cap(-B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
\mu\equiv 0
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{\geq}_{n}\cap(-B^{\geq}_{n})=R_{n}^{+}\cap R_{n}^{-}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in R_{n}^{+}\setminus R_{n}^{-}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\geq}
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in R_{n}^{-}\setminus R_{n}^{+}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
\mu\in-\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\geq}
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{\geq}_{n}\setminus R_{n}^{+}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}
.

•

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in(-B^{\geq}_{n})\setminus R_{n}^{-}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
\mu\in-\mathcal{M}_{n}^{+}
.

Proof.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}\in(B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}\cap(-B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}
. By Theorem
9
, and by the fact that
F
is a bijective linear map, we have that
(B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}\cap(-B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}=F^{-1}((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1})^{\mathsf{c}}\cap(\mathbb{R}_{-}^{n-1})^{\mathsf{c}})
. Thus, there are
a,b\in[n-1]
such that
\phi_{a}>0
and
\phi_{b}<0
. If
\mu
is not identically zero, then by Lemma
B.1
there are distinct
i,j\in[n]
such that
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}\neq 0
. We can now find
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
such that
\sigma_{a}=i=\pi_{b},\sigma_{a+1}=j=\pi_{b+1}
and thus

\min({\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1}),{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1}))=\min(\phi_{a}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}),\phi_{b}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}))<0

which is impossible if
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric. Thus, if
{\bm{\beta}}\in(B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}\cap(-B^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}
, then
\mu\equiv 0
.

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in B_{n}^{\geq}\cap(-B_{n}^{\geq})=F^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n-1}\cap\mathbb{R}_{-}^{n-1})=F^{-1}(\{\mathbf{0}\})=\mathbf{\{}0\}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv 0
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and hence it is always a semimetric.

In all the remaining case we always have that
{\bm{\beta}}\neq\mathbf{0}
. On the one hand if
{\bm{\beta}}\in R_{+}\setminus R_{-}\sqcup B^{\geq}_{n}\setminus R_{+}
, the non-negativity of
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
requires
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}\geq 0
for all distinct
i,j\in[n]
, that is
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
. Indeed, since
{\bm{\beta}}\neq\mathbf{0}
, there exists
a\in[n-1]
such that
\phi_{a}>0
, by Theorem
9
. For all distinct
i,j\in[n]
, there exists
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
such that
\sigma_{a}=i,\sigma_{a+1}=j
, and hence

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=(\mu(\sigma_{a})+\mu(\sigma_{a+1}))\phi_{a}.

On the other hand, by an analogous argument, if
{\bm{\beta}}\in R_{-}\setminus R_{+}\sqcup(-B^{\geq}_{n})\setminus R_{-}
, the non-negativity of
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
requires
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}\leq 0
for all distinct
i,j\in[n]
, that is
\mu\in-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
.
Therefore in the last four cases of the Lemma,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is non-negative if and only if
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
. From now on we assume that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
. Next we consider triangle inequality. Since
n>2
, we have that for all
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)-{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|A|}\left(\mu(\bigtriangleup_{A}(\sigma,\omega))+\mu(\bigtriangleup_{A}(\omega,\pi))-\mu(\bigtriangleup_{A}(\sigma,\pi))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|A|}\begin{cases}2\mu(M(A,\omega))&\textrm{ if }|\{M(A,\sigma),M(A,\omega),M(A,\pi)\}|=3\\
2\left(\mu(M(A,\omega))+\mu(M(A,\sigma))\right)&\textrm{ if }M(A,\sigma)=M(A,\pi)\neq M(A,\omega)\\
0&\textrm{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in R_{n}^{+}\setminus R^{-}_{n}\sqcup R_{n}^{-}\setminus R^{+}_{n}
, then any non-negative
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
satisfies triangle inequality because condition
|\{M(A,\sigma),M(A,\omega),M(A,\pi)\}|=3
cannot be satisfied by sets
A
of cardinality
2
. Therefore, the third and fourth bullet points of the lemma are fullfilled.

Assume now that
{\bm{\beta}}\in B_{n}^{\geq}\setminus R^{+}_{n}\sqcup(-B_{n}^{\geq})\setminus R^{-}_{n}
. Let
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
\pi
be its antipodal permutation. Then, for any
A\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we have
M(A,\sigma)\neq M(A,\pi)
, thus

\left(\mu(\bigtriangleup_{A}(\sigma,\omega))+\mu(\bigtriangleup_{A}(\omega,\pi))-\mu(\bigtriangleup_{A}(\sigma,\pi))\right)\neq 0

iff
M(A,\sigma),M(A,\pi)
, and
M(A,\omega)
are all distinct. Let
a\in[2,n]
. If
\omega=\sigma t_{a-1,a}
, then
M(A,\sigma)=M(A,\omega)
for all
A\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, except those
A
containing both
\sigma_{a-1}
and
\sigma_{a}
but no
\sigma_{j}
for
j<a-1
.
Overall we have
\binom{n-a}{k-2}
sets of size
k
with this property. Moreover, for all these sets we have that
M(A,\omega)\neq M(A,\pi)
except for the case in which
A=\{\sigma_{a-1},\sigma_{a}\}
. Thus, we have that

(16)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)-{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=2\mu(\sigma_{a})\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}.

If
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{\geq}_{n}\setminus R^{+}_{n}
,
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}
is a semimetric and thus, by triangle inequality,

\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\omega)+\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\omega,\pi)-\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\pi)=2\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}\geq 0

and hence
\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}\geq 0
. Notice that the map
(\beta_{k})_{k=3}^{n}\mapsto\left(\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}\right)_{a=2}^{n-1}
is bijective and thus the quantities
\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}
are all zero iff
(\beta_{k})_{k=3}^{n}\equiv 0
. However, this is impossible since we are assuming
{\bm{\beta}}\in B_{n}^{\geq}\setminus R^{+}_{n}=B_{n}^{\geq}\setminus(R^{+}_{n}\cup R^{-}_{n})
. Thus, there is some
a\in[2,n-1]
such that
\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}>0
.

Specularly, since
{\bm{\beta}}\in(-B^{\geq}_{n})\setminus R^{-}_{n}
, then
-{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{\geq}_{n}\setminus R^{+}_{n}
and
\mathrm{d}_{-{\bm{\beta}}}=-\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}
is a semimetric, we conclude that there is some
a\in[2,n-1]
such that

\sum_{k=3}^{n+2-a}\binom{n-a}{k-2}\beta_{k}<0
.

In conclusion, if
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{\geq}_{n}\setminus R^{+}_{n}
(or
(-B^{\geq}_{n})\setminus R^{-}_{n}
), by (
16
), and the arbitrariety of
\sigma
, we have that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
satisfies the triangle inequality if and only if
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
(or
\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
).
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose
n>2
. Given
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

•

If
\mu\in(\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}\cap(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})^{\mathsf{c}}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}=\mathbf{0}
.

•

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\cap(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

•

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}\setminus(-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n})
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}\in B^{\geq}_{n}
.

•

If
\mu\in(-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n})\setminus\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}\in-B^{\geq}_{n}
.

•

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}\in R^{+}_{n}
.

•

If
\mu\in(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})\setminus(-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n})
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}\in R^{-}_{n}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric. If
\mu\notin(\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\cup-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})
, then there exist distinct
i,j\in[n]
such that
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}<0
and distinct
k,\ell\in[n]
such that
\mu_{k}+\mu_{\ell}>0
. Let now
a\in\argmax_{c\in[n-1]}\phi_{c}
and
b\in\argmin_{c\in[n-1]}\phi_{c}
.
Let now
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
be such that
\sigma_{a}=i
and
\sigma_{a+1}=j
. Then,

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\phi_{a}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j})

is non-negative if and only if
\phi_{a}\leq 0
. Similarly, if
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
is such that
\sigma_{b}=k
and
\sigma_{b+1}=\ell
. Then,

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{b,b+1})=\phi_{b}(\mu_{k}+\mu_{\ell})

is non-negative iff
\phi_{b}\geq 0
. Thus, if
\mu\notin(\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\cup-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})
, then
\phi\equiv 0
and, by Theorem
9
,
{\bm{\beta}}=\mathbf{0}
.

Suppose now that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\cap-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
, then, by Lemma
B.1
,
\mu\equiv 0
and thus any
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
will make
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
the trivial semimetric.

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}=\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}\setminus(-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n})\sqcup\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}
, then for any distinct
i,j\in[n]
,
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}\geq 0
and there are some distinct
i,j\in[n]
such that
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}>0
. Now, for any
a\in[n-1]
, there is some
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
such that
\sigma_{a}=i
and
\sigma_{a+1}=j
. Therefore,

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\phi_{a}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j})\geq 0,

implies that
\phi_{a}\geq 0
for any
a\in[n-1]
and thus,
{\bm{\beta}}
must be in
B^{\geq}_{n}
.
Thus, we have shown that in cases 3 and 5, if
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric, then
{\bm{\beta}}
must be in
B^{\geq}_{n}
. Applying Lemma
B.2
, we conclude that this is sufficient in case 3, and that under case 5,
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric iff
{\bm{\beta}}\in R^{+}_{n}
.

Similarly, if
\mu\in(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}=(-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n})\setminus\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n}\sqcup(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n})\setminus(-\mathcal{M}^{+}_{n})
, we conclude that under cases 4 and 6, if
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric, then
{\bm{\beta}}
must be in
-B^{\geq}_{n}
. By Lemma
B.2
, we complete these two cases.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Suppose first that
n=2
. If
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric, then we have three cases. If
\beta_{2}>0
, then we must have that
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}\geq 0
, and hence
({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in R_{2}^{++}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{2}
. If
\beta_{2}<0
, then
\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}\leq 0
, and hence
({\bm{\beta}},\mu)\in R^{--}_{2}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}(-\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{2})
. If
\beta_{2}=0
, then
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\equiv 0
for all
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The converse inclusion readily follows.

Suppose
n>2
. The claim follows by Lemmas
B.2
and
B.3
.∎

C.
Proofs in §
4.3

Proof of Proposition
2
.

One implication is straightforward. Suppose that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}=\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mu}
for some
{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. Then, for all
a\in[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

\phi^{\bm{\beta}}_{a}(\mu(\sigma_{a})+\mu(\sigma_{a+1}))={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\mu}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\phi^{\bm{\gamma}}_{a}(\mu(\sigma_{a})+\mu(\sigma_{a+1})).

Since, for every
a\in[n-1]
there is some
\sigma
such that
\mu(\sigma_{a})+\mu(\sigma_{a+1})\neq 0
, we conclude that
\phi_{a}^{\bm{\beta}}=\phi_{a}^{\bm{\gamma}}
for all
a\in[n-1]
. By Theorem
D.8
this implies that
{\bm{\beta}}={\bm{\gamma}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3
.

One implication is straightforward. For any
a\in[n-1]
,

\phi_{a}(\mu(\sigma_{a})+\mu(\sigma_{a+1}))={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}^{\nu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\phi_{a}(\nu(\sigma_{a})+\nu(\sigma_{a+1})).

Since
\mathrm{d}_{{\bm{\beta}}}
or
\mathrm{d}_{-{\bm{\beta}}}
is a metric,
|\phi_{a}|=|\mathrm{d}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})|/2>0
for all
a\in[n-1]
. Therefore
\mu(i)+\mu(j)=\nu(i)+\nu(j)
for any distinct
i,j\in[n]
. Thus, in particular, for any distinct
i,j,k\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\mu(i)+\mu(j)=\nu(i)+\nu(j),\,\,\,\mu(i)+\mu(k)=\nu(i)+\nu(k),\,\,\,\mu(j)+\mu(k)=\nu(j)+\nu(k)

Summing the first two equations and subtracting the last, we get that
\mu(i)=\nu(i)
. By the arbitrariness of
i,j,k
, the proof is concluded.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

Let
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
be neutral, then for any
a\in[n-2]
, we have that

(17)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\mathrm{I},t_{a+1,a+2})={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(t_{a,a+1},t_{a,a+1}t_{a+1,a+2})={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(t_{a,a+2},t_{a,a+2}t_{a+1,a+2})

But

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\mathrm{I},t_{a+1,a+2})

\displaystyle=\phi_{a+1}(\mu(a+1)+\mu(a+2))

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(t_{a,a+1},t_{a,a+1}t_{a+1,a+2})

\displaystyle=\phi_{a+1}(\mu(a)+\mu(a+2))

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(t_{a,a+2},t_{a,a+2}t_{a+1,a+2})

\displaystyle=\phi_{a+1}(\mu(a)+\mu(a+1))

Since
\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}
or
\mathrm{d}_{-{\bm{\beta}}}
is a metric, we have that
|\phi_{a+1}|=|\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathrm{I},t_{a+1,a+2})|/2>0
. Therefore, by (
17
) it follows that
\mu(a)=\mu(a+1)=\mu(a+2)
. By the arbitrariness of
a
, we obtain that
\mu
is constant. To show the converse, fix
S\subseteq[n]
,
\sigma,\pi,\omega\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. We have that,
M(\sigma(S),\sigma\pi)=\sigma(M(S,\pi))
. Indeed,

\displaystyle M(\sigma(S),\sigma\pi)

\displaystyle=\{x\in\sigma(S):\not\exists y\in\sigma(S)\textrm{ s.t. }y>_{\sigma\pi}x\}

\displaystyle=\{\sigma_{z}\in\sigma(S):\not\exists\sigma_{w}\in\sigma(S)\textrm{ s.t. }\sigma_{w}>_{\sigma\pi}\sigma_{z}\}

\displaystyle=\{\sigma_{z}\in\sigma(S):\not\exists w\in S\textrm{ s.t. }w>_{\pi}z\}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left(\{z\in S:\not\exists w\in S\textrm{ s.t. }w>_{\pi}z\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma\left(M(S,\pi)\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\bigtriangleup_{\sigma(S)}(\sigma\pi,\sigma\omega)

\displaystyle=M(\sigma(S),\sigma\pi)\bigtriangleup M(\sigma(S),\sigma\omega)=\sigma(M(S,\pi))\bigtriangleup\sigma(M(S,\omega))

\displaystyle=\sigma(M(S,\pi)\bigtriangleup M(S,\omega))=\sigma(\bigtriangleup_{S}(\pi,\omega)).

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma\pi,\sigma\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|S|}|\bigtriangleup_{S}(\sigma\pi,\sigma\omega)|=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|S|}|\bigtriangleup_{\sigma(\sigma^{-1}(S))}(\sigma\pi,\sigma\omega)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|\sigma^{-1}(S)|}|\sigma(\bigtriangleup_{\sigma^{-1}(S)}(\sigma,\pi))|=\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\beta_{|S^{\prime}|}|\bigtriangleup_{S^{\prime}}(\pi,\omega)|=\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}(\pi,\omega).

∎

D.
Proofs in §
4.4

The following two auxiliary lemmas are immediate observations.

Lemma D.1
.

For all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
,

\{\{i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t})\}:k\in[\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)]\}=\{\{i,j\}:i<_{\sigma}j\textrm{ and }i>_{\pi}j\}.

Lemma D.2
.

For all distinct
i,j\in[n]
, the number of couples
(\sigma,\pi)\in\mathbb{S}_{n}^{2}
with
I(\sigma,\pi)=\{(i,j)\}
is
(n-1)!
. Moreover,
|\sigma^{-1}_{j}-\sigma^{-1}_{i}|=1
and
\pi=\sigma t_{i,j}=t_{\sigma^{-1}_{i},\sigma^{-1}_{j}}\sigma
.

The following lemma is essentially due to
[
31
]
(Lemma 2) and it can be proved analogously.

Lemma D.3
.

For all
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
we have that

\left(\sigma\prod_{j=1}^{k}t_{a_{j}(\mathbf{t}),a_{j}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right)_{k=0}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}

are on a line.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
\mathrm{d}
be a semimetric satisfying axiom
A.1
.
For any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
with,
I(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=I(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=\{(i,j)\}
we have that
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

If
n\leq 2
, the claim is trivial. Without loss of generality, by Lemma
D.2
, there are
a,b\in[n-1]
such that

\sigma_{a}=i,\ \sigma_{a+1}=j,\ \textnormal{and}\ \pi_{b}=i,\pi_{b+1}=j.

First assume that
n>3
and that
\pi=t_{k,l}\sigma
,
for some distinct
k,l
both different from
i,j
. Then, we have the following betweenness relations

\begin{matrix}&&\sigma&&\\
&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\diagup}&&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\diagdown}&\\
t_{i,j}\sigma&&&&t_{k,l}\sigma\\
&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\diagdown}&&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\diagup}&\\
&&t_{i,j}t_{k,l}\sigma&&\end{matrix}

Thus, by Axiom
A.1
we have that,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{i,j}t_{k,l}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{i,j}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{i,j}\sigma,t_{i,j}t_{k,l}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{k,l}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{k,l}\sigma,t_{i,j}t_{k,l}\sigma),

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(t_{i,j}\sigma,t_{k,l}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(t_{i,j}\sigma,\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{k,l}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(t_{i,j}\sigma,t_{i,j}t_{k,l}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{i,j}t_{k,l}\sigma,t_{k,l}\sigma).

This system of equations implies that
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{i,j}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(\pi,t_{i,j}\pi)
.

Iteratively, we have that if
\pi=\left(\coprod_{k=1}^{m}t_{i_{k},j_{k}}\right)\sigma
where
i_{k},j_{k}\notin\{i,j\}
for all
k\in[m]
, then

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{i,j}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\sigma,t_{i,j}t_{i_{1},j_{1}}\sigma)=\ldots=\mathrm{d}(\pi,t_{i,j}\pi).

This shows that if
\pi
is a permutation with
\sigma_{a}=\pi_{a}=i
and
\sigma_{a+1}=\pi_{a+1}=j
, then
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{i,j}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(\pi,t_{i,j}\pi)
. That is, the claim holds with
a=b
. Indeed, in such case
\pi
can be obtained transforming
\sigma
by finitely many transpositions
(t_{i_{k},j_{k}})_{k=1}^{m}
with
i_{k},j_{k}\notin\left\{i,j\right\}
.

Now we consider
a\neq b
and allow for
n\geq 3
. We start assuming that
b=a+1
. Let
i=\sigma_{a}
,
j=\sigma_{a+1}
, and
k=\sigma_{a+2}
. From the definition of betweenness, we have that

\begin{matrix}&&\underset{ijk}{\sigma}&&\\
&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\diagup}&&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\diagdown}\\
\underset{jik}{t_{ij}\sigma}&&&&\underset{ikj}{t_{jk}\sigma}\\
\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|&&&&\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\\
\underset{jki}{t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma}&&&&\underset{kij}{t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma}\\
&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\diagdown}&&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\diagup}\\
&&\underset{kji}{t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma}&&\end{matrix}

Thus, by Axiom
A.1
we have that,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma,t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma,t_{ij}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{ij}\sigma),

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(t_{ij}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(t_{ij}\sigma,\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(t_{ij}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma,t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma),

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(t_{jk}\sigma,\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{ij}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ij}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma)+\mathrm{d}(t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ik}t_{ij}\sigma).

From this linear system, we conclude that

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{ij}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma,t_{ij}t_{ik}t_{jk}\sigma).

If
n=3
, then this equality yields the claim. Instead, whenever
n>3
, if
\sigma
is any permutation with
\sigma_{a}=i
,
\sigma_{a+1}=j
, and if
\pi
is any permutation with
\pi_{b}=i
,
\pi_{b+1}=j
, then by "hexagonal moves" we can obtain recursively
\tau
such that
\tau_{b}=i
and
\tau_{b+1}=j
and such that
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,t_{ij}\sigma)=\mathrm{d}(\tau,t_{ij}\tau)
.
18
18

18

More specifically assuming wlog that
b>a
, we can take

\tau=\left(\coprod_{s=1}^{b-a}t_{i,\sigma_{a+1+s}}t_{j,\sigma_{a+1+s}}\right)\sigma

which has

\tau_{b}=i
and
\tau_{b+1}=j
.
Now, by the previous argument (case
a=b
above), we have that
\mathrm{d}(\tau,t_{ij}\tau)=\mathrm{d}(\pi,t_{ij}\pi)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5
.

For all distinct
i,j\in\left[n\right]
, let
g(i,j):=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)
for any
\sigma,\pi
with
I(\sigma,\pi)=\{(i,j)\}
. We let
g(i,i):=0
otherwise. By Lemma
D.4
, this
g:[n]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a well-defined symmetric function. Finally, by Axiom
A.1
, Lemmas
D.1
, and
D.3
, we obtain that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
,

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}g(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))=\sum_{(i,j)\in I(\sigma,\pi)}g(i,j).

Conversely, suppose that
\sigma-\omega-\pi
for some
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Then, there is a
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
and a
\ell\leq\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)
such that
\omega=\sigma\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}t_{a_{j}(\mathbf{t}),a_{j}(\mathbf{t})+1}
and
\pi=\omega\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}t_{a_{j}(\mathbf{t}),a_{j}(\mathbf{t})+1}
. Thus,

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}g(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}g(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\sum_{k=\ell+1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}g(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}),j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\omega)+\mathrm{d}(\omega,\pi).

∎

Expansion on Remark
6
.

By Theorem
5
it is sufficient to show that if

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\omega)+\mathrm{d}(\omega,\pi)=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)
for some
\sigma,\omega,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, then
\sigma-\omega-\pi
. Given that,

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\omega)=\sum_{(i,j)\in I(\sigma,\omega)}g(i,j)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathrm{d}(\omega,\pi)=\sum_{(i,j)\in I(\omega,\pi)}g(i,j)

were
\omega
not in between
\sigma
and
\pi
, we would have
I(\sigma,\pi)\subsetneq I(\sigma,\omega)\cup I(\omega,\pi)
.
But this is impossible, since
g
only takes positive values.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7
.

By Theorem
5
it is sufficient to show that
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axiom
A.2
if and only if there exists a
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
such that
g(i,j)=\mu(i)+\mu(j)
for all distinct
i,j\in[n]
. If
n<4
, then Axiom
A.2
holds vacuously and indeed
g(i,j)
can be written as a sum. In particular, if
n=3
, taking

\mu(1)=\frac{g(1,2)+g(1,3)-g(2,3)}{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\mu(2)=\frac{g(1,2)-g(1,3)+g(2,3)}{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\mu(3)=\frac{-g(1,2)+g(1,3)+g(2,3)}{2}.

we have that
g(i,j)=\mu(i)+\mu(j)
for all
i,j\in[n]
. If
n\geq 4
, we define
\mu(i):=(g(i,j)-g(k,j)+g(i,k))/2
for some distinct
j,k
both different from
i
. Under Axiom
A.2
, for all
i,j,k,\ell
with
i\neq j
,
k\neq\ell
,
i\neq k
, and
j\neq\ell
,

g(i,j)+g(k,\ell)=g(i,k)+g(j,\ell).

This implies that
\mu
is well defined. Indeed, if
k^{\prime},j^{\prime}
are distinct and different from
i
, then

\displaystyle\left(g(i,j)-g(k,j)+g(i,k)\right)-\left(g(i,j^{\prime})-g(k^{\prime},j^{\prime})+g(i,k^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(g(i,j)+g(k^{\prime},j^{\prime})\right)-\left(g(i,j^{\prime})+g(k,j)\right)+g(i,k)-g(i,k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(g(i,k^{\prime})+g(j,j^{\prime})\right)-\left(g(i,k)+g(j,j^{\prime})\right)+g(i,k)-g(i,k^{\prime})=0

Moreover, we have that for all
i\neq j

\displaystyle g(i,j)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(g(i,j)-g(k,j)+g(i,k)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(g(j,i)-g(k,i)+g(j,k)\right)=\mu(i)+\mu(j).

The converse is straightforward.
∎

Proposition D.5
.

A semimetric
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axiom
A.3
if and only if, for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

(18)

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{j=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right).

Proof.

If
\sigma,\pi
have Kendall distance lower or equal than one, the claim is trivial. Suppose that the claim holds for all couples
\sigma,\pi
with
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)<m
and let
\sigma,\pi
have distance
m
. By the axiom
A.3
there is some
\omega
lying between
\sigma,\pi
such that
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\omega)+\mathrm{d}(\omega,\pi)=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)
. But clearly,
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\omega),\mathrm{d}_{K}(\omega,\pi)<m
, thus by inductive hypothesis there are some
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in T(\sigma,\omega)
and
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}\in T(\omega,\pi)
, such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\omega)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})+1}\right)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\omega,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime})+1}\right).

Denote by
\mathbf{\tilde{t}}
the concatenation of
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\mathrm{d}_{K}
is a metric and
\sigma-\omega-\pi
, we have that
\tilde{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}})+1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right).

The reverse inequality follows from triangle inequality. Conversely, if (
18
) holds and

\mathbf{t}^{*}\in\argmin_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right),

then for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)\}
we have that by Lemma
D.3

\omega^{j}:=\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{*}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{*})+1}

lies between
\sigma
and
\pi
. We need to prove that
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\omega^{j})+\mathrm{d}(\omega^{j},\pi)=\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)
. To prove this, we need to show that
\mathbf{t}^{{}^{\prime}j}:=\mathbf{t}^{*}|_{\{1,\ldots,j\}}
and
\mathbf{t}^{{}^{\prime\prime}j}:=\mathbf{t}^{*}|_{\{j+1,\ldots,\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)\}}
are such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{{}^{\prime}j}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{t\in T(\sigma,\omega^{j})}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{t^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}^{j}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{t\in T(\omega^{j},\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)-j}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right).

Indeed, assume they are not and
\mathbf{u}^{{}^{\prime}j}
is such that

\sum_{k=1}^{j}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{\prime}}^{j}),a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{\prime}}^{j})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{\prime}}^{j}),a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{\prime}}^{j})+1}\right)<\sum_{k=1}^{j}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{\prime}}^{j}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{\prime}}^{j})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{\prime}}^{j}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{\prime}}^{j})+1}\right),

then by concatenating
\mathbf{u}^{\prime j}
with
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime j}
, we would get a new element
\mathbf{u}^{*}
of
T(\sigma,\pi)
with

\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{*}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{*}})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{*}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{u^{*}})+1}\right)<\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{*}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{*}})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{*}}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t^{*}})+1}\right)

contradicting the minimality of
\mathbf{t}^{*}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8
.

By Proposition
D.5
, we have that Axiom
A.3
yields

\mathrm{d}:(\sigma,\pi)\mapsto\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\mathrm{d}\left(\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{k=1}^{j}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right).

By Axiom
A.4
, for all
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,
a\in\left[n-1\right]
and
i,j\in\left[n\right]
, if
\omega^{\prime}=\omega t_{a,a+1}=t_{i,j}\omega
, we can define
g_{a}(i,j):=\mathrm{d}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
. Therefore, we can rewrite the previous expression for
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)
as in (
3
). Now we prove the converse. Notice that Axiom
A.3
holds by Proposition
D.5
. Passing to Axiom
A.4
let
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
be such that

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\pi,\pi_{a,a+1})=\left\{(i,j)\right\}.

Then,

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=g_{a}(i,j)=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1}).

∎

Theorem D.6
.

A semimetric
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axioms
A.3
,
A.5
, and
A.6
if and only if there are
{\bm{\phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
such that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
,

(19)

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\pi)=\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}\left(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t}))\right).

Moreover,
\mu
can be chosen so that
\sum_{i}\mu(i)=1
.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{d}\equiv 0
there is nothing to show. Therefore we assume that
\mathrm{d}
is not identically zero.

If
n\geq 4
, notice that
A.6
implies
A.4
. Indeed, let
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
a\in[n-1]
such that

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1}).

Let
\rho\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
be such that
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})\cap I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})=\emptyset
and
I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})\cap I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})=\emptyset
. Then, by Axiom
A.6
, we have that

\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})

and hence Axiom
A.4
holds.
Therefore, by Theorem
8
it is sufficient to prove that
\mathrm{d}
satisfies axioms
A.5
and
A.6
if and only if there are
{\bm{\phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{\geq}_{n}
such that for all
a\in[n-1]
and distinct
i,j\in[n]
,

g_{a}(i,j)=(\mu(i)+\mu(j))\phi_{a}.

From Axiom
A.6
, we have that for all
a\in[n-1]
, and all distinct
i,j,k,\ell\in[n]
,

g_{a}(i,j)+g_{a}(k,\ell)=g_{a}(i,k)+g_{a}(j,\ell).

We now define

\mu_{a}(i)=(g_{a}(i,j)-g_{a}(k,j)+g_{a}(i,k))/2
for some distinct
j,k
both different from
i
. This is well defined because if
k^{\prime},j^{\prime}
are also distinct and different from
i
, then

\displaystyle\left(g_{a}(i,j)-g_{a}(k,j)+g_{a}(i,k)\right)-\left(g_{a}(i,j^{\prime})-g_{a}(k^{\prime},j^{\prime})+g_{a}(i,k^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(g_{a}(i,j)+g_{a}(k^{\prime},j^{\prime})\right)-\left(g_{a}(i,j^{\prime})+g_{a}(k,j)\right)+g_{a}(i,k)-g_{a}(i,k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(g_{a}(i,k^{\prime})+g_{a}(j,j^{\prime})\right)-\left(g_{a}(i,k)+g_{a}(j,j^{\prime})\right)+g_{a}(i,k)-g_{a}(i,k^{\prime})=0

But then, we have that for distinct
i,j\in[n]
,

\displaystyle g_{a}(i,j)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(g_{a}(i,j)-g_{a}(k,j)+g_{a}(i,k)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(g_{a}(j,i)-g_{a}(k,i)+g_{a}(j,k)\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{a}(i)+\mu_{a}(j).

Now we define
\mu:i\mapsto\mu_{a}(i)/\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{a}(j)
. This is well defined because of Axiom
A.5
. Indeed, Axiom
A.5
yields that

g_{a}(i,j)g_{b}(k,\ell)=(\mu_{a}(i)+\mu_{a}(j))(\mu_{b}(k)+\mu_{b}(\ell))=(\mu_{b}(i)+\mu_{b}(j))(\mu_{a}(k)+\mu_{a}(\ell))=g_{a}(k,\ell)g_{b}(i,j).

Thus, since
\mathrm{d}
is not identically zero, there exist
x\in[n-1]
and distinct
p,q\in[n]
such that
g_{x}(p,q)>0
. Moreover, in this case we must also have that
\mu_{x}([n])=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{(i,j)}g_{x}(i,j)\geq\frac{g_{x}(p,q)}{n-1}>0
.
Thus, we define

c_{a}:=\frac{g_{a}(p,q)}{g_{x}(p,q)},

then

g_{a}(i,j)=\mu_{a}(i)+\mu_{a}(j)=c_{a}(\mu_{x}(i)+\mu_{x}(j))
. Now if
i,j,k
are all distinct, we get the system

\displaystyle\mu_{a}(i)+\mu_{a}(j)

\displaystyle=c_{a}(\mu_{x}(i)+\mu_{x}(j))

\displaystyle\mu_{a}(i)+\mu_{a}(k)

\displaystyle=c_{a}(\mu_{x}(i)+\mu_{x}(k))

\displaystyle\mu_{a}(j)+\mu_{a}(k)

\displaystyle=c_{a}(\mu_{x}(j)+\mu_{x}(k))

Summing the first two and subtracting the last equation, we get
\mu_{a}(i)=c_{a}\mu_{x}(i)
. This shows that
\mu
is well defined independently of
a
and that it sums up to 1. Finally, we can define
\phi_{a}:=\mu_{a}([n])/\mu([n])
. So that we conclude that
g_{a}(i,j)=\mu_{a}(i)+\mu_{a}(j)=\phi_{a}(\mu(i)+\mu(j))
.

If
n<3
, then all the Axioms hold vacuously and such does the claim. Instead if
n=3
, then Axioms
A.4
and
A.6
still hold vacuously, and by Axiom
A.3
there is some
g
such that
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=g_{a}(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{a+1})
for all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
a\in[n-1]
. We define
\mu_{x}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{x}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{x}(1,2)+g_{x}(1,3)-g_{x}(2,3)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{x}(2)

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{x}(1,2)-g_{x}(1,3)+g_{x}(2,3)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{x}(3)

\displaystyle=\frac{-g_{x}(1,2)+g_{x}(1,3)+g_{x}(2,3)}{2}.

Since
\mathrm{d}
is not trivial, there exist
x\in[n-1]
and distinct
p,q\in[n]
, such that
g_{x}(p,q)>0
. This implies that
\mu_{x}([3])>0
, thus we can define
\mu:=\mu_{x}/\mu_{x}([3])
. Finally we define

\phi_{x}:=\mu_{x}([3])
and
\phi_{y}:=\phi_{x}g_{y}(p,q)/g_{x}(p,q)
where
y\in[1,2]\setminus\{x\}
. Then we have that

g_{x}(i,j)=\phi_{x}(\mu(i)+\mu(j))
for any distinct
i,j\in[3]
. Moreover, by Axiom
A.5
,

g_{y}(i,j)=\frac{g_{y}(p,q)}{g_{x}(p,q)}g_{x}(i,j)=\frac{g_{y}(p,q)}{g_{x}(p,q)}\phi_{x}(\mu(i)+\mu(j))=\phi_{y}(\mu(i)+\mu(j)).

Therefore, the proof is concluded.
∎

Lemma D.7
.

If
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a semimetric, then it satisfies Axiom
A.3
.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. If
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)\leq 1
, there is nothing to prove. Therefore, suppose that
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)\geq 2
and let
X:=\{i\in[n-1]:\sigma_{i+1}>_{\pi}\sigma_{i}\}
. Let
\sigma^{i}=\sigma t_{i,i+1}
and define
\chi:i\mapsto{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma^{i})+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma^{i},\pi)-{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)
. We want to find
i\in[n-1]
such that
\chi(i)=0
.
Here we display the three orders that we are considering

\begin{matrix}\sigma&=&\sigma_{1}&\ldots&\sigma_{i-1}&\sigma_{i}&\sigma_{i+1}&\sigma_{i+2}&\ldots&\sigma_{n}\\
\sigma^{i}&=&\sigma_{1}&\ldots&\sigma_{i-1}&\sigma_{i+1}&\sigma_{i}&\sigma_{i+2}&\ldots&\sigma_{n}\\
\pi&=&\pi_{1}&\ldots&\pi_{i-1}&\pi_{i}&\pi_{i+1}&\pi_{i+2}&\ldots&\pi_{n}\\
\end{matrix}

From this, it is easy to verify that for all
i\in[n-1]
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\sigma^{i})=

\displaystyle(\mu(\sigma_{i})+\mu(\sigma_{i+1}))\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)\right)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma^{i},\pi)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq i,i+1}\mu(\sigma_{j})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-j)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{j}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(j,n]}\cap\sigma_{j}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mu(\sigma_{i})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mu(\sigma_{i+1})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{\{i\}\cup(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)\right)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu(\sigma_{j})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-j)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{j}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(j,n]}\cap\sigma_{j}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)\right).

We have that
\chi
is given by

\displaystyle\chi(i)=

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma_{i})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)\right)+

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma_{i})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)\right)+

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma_{i})\left(2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i,n]}\cap\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)\right)+

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma_{i+1})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)\right)+

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma_{i+1})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{\{i\}\cup(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)\right)+

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma_{i+1})\left(2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\mu(\sigma_{i})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i,n]}\cap\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)\right)+

\displaystyle 2\mu(\sigma_{i+1})\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-i-1)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|\sigma_{\{i\}\cup(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|\right)\right).

We need to find
i^{*}\in X
such that
\sigma_{i^{*}+1}>_{\pi}\sigma_{j}
for all
j>i^{*}+1
and for
j=i^{*}
. Indeed, if this is the case, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{(i^{*}+1,n]}

\displaystyle\subseteq\sigma_{\{i^{*}\}\cup(i^{*}+1,n]}\subseteq\sigma_{i^{*}+1}^{\downarrow\pi},

which implies

\left|\sigma_{(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|=n-i-1,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{ and }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left|\sigma_{\{i\}\cup(i+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i+1}^{\downarrow\pi}\right|=n-i.

Moreover, if
\sigma_{i^{*}+1}>_{\pi}\sigma_{i^{*}}
we also have that

\left|\sigma_{(i^{*},n]}\cap\sigma_{i^{*}}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|=\left|\sigma_{(i^{*}+1,n]}\cap\sigma_{i^{*}}^{\downarrow,\pi}\right|.

Thus, finding such
i^{*}
would conclude the proof. In particular, for
i^{*}=\max X
, we have indeed that
\sigma_{i^{*}+1}>_{\pi}\sigma_{i^{*}}
because
i^{*}\in X
. Finally, because of maximality, for all
j\geq i^{*}+1
, we must have that
\sigma_{j}>_{\pi}\sigma_{j+1}
and thus,

\sigma_{i^{*}+1}>_{\pi}\sigma_{i^{*}+2}>_{\pi}\ldots>_{\pi}\sigma_{n-1}>_{\pi}\sigma_{n}.

Therefore,
\chi(i^{*})=0
and hence we have that there exists
\omega=\sigma^{i^{*}}
such that
\sigma-\omega-\pi
and

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)

proving that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
satisfies Axiom
A.3
.
∎

Given a vector
{\bm{\phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the map
D_{{\bm{\phi}}}^{\mu}:\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
as

D_{{\bm{\phi}}}^{\mu}:(\sigma,\pi)\mapsto\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}_{k=1}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}.

Theorem D.8
.

If
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is a non-trivial semimetric, then there is a unique
{\bm{\phi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
such that

(20)

\forall\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n},\,\,\,\,\,\,{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=D_{{\bm{\phi}}}^{\mu}(\sigma,\pi).

Conversely, if
D^{\mu}_{\bm{\phi}}
is a non-trivial semimetric, there is a unique
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
such that holds (
20
).

Proof.

We define the function
a\mapsto\phi_{a}
, by

(21)

\phi_{a}:=f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-a)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-a-1)=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\beta_{k}\binom{n-a-1}{k-2}.

Assume that
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)=1
, then
\pi=\sigma t_{a,a+1}=t_{i,j}\sigma
. In this case,

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=(\mu(i)+\mu(j))(f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-a)-f_{\bm{\beta}}(n-a-1))=(\mu(i)+\mu(j))\phi_{a}.

As
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
is not identically zero, there exist
x\in[n-1]
and
\sigma,\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
with
\pi=\sigma t_{x,x+1}
such that

(\mu(\sigma_{x})+\mu(\sigma_{x+1}))\phi_{x}={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)>0.

For all
a\in[n-1]
, there exist
\rho,\tau\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
such that
\tau=\rho t_{a,a+1}
and
\rho_{a}=\sigma_{x}
and
\rho_{a+1}=\sigma_{x+1}

and then, as
\mu(\sigma_{x})+\mu(\sigma_{x+1})\neq 0
. Therefore, for all
a\in[n-1]
,
\phi_{a}
is pinned down uniquely. Indeed, suppose that there exist
\tilde{{\bm{\phi}}}
such that (
20
) holds. Then, for all
a\in[n-1]
,

(\mu(\sigma_{x})+\mu(\sigma_{x+1}))\phi_{a}=(\mu(\sigma_{x})+\mu(\sigma_{x+1}))\tilde{\phi}_{a}

and hence,
\phi_{a}=\tilde{\phi}_{a}
. This shows that (
20
) holds for
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)=1
. Now suppose that
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)>1
and let
\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
. Then

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,\sigma)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\left(\sigma\prod_{h=1}^{k-1}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1},\sigma\prod_{h=1}^{k}t_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}),a_{k}(\mathbf{t})+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}

which yields that

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)\leq\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}.

Now assume that (
20
) holds for any couple of permutations with Kendall distance less than
m
, and suppose that
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)=m
. By Lemma
D.7
, there is some
\omega
in between
\sigma
and
\pi
such that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)
. Clearly,
\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\omega),\mathrm{d}_{K}(\omega,\pi)<m
, thus by inductive hypothesis, there exist
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in T(\sigma,\omega)
and
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}\in T(\omega,\pi)
, such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\omega)}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\omega,\pi)}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime})}

which yields, by denoting
\mathbf{\tilde{t}}
the concatenation of
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{t}^{\prime\prime}
,

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,\pi)=\sum_{k=1}^{\mathrm{d}_{K}(\sigma,\pi)}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{\tilde{t}})}\geq\min_{\mathbf{t}\in T(\sigma,\pi)}\sum_{k=1}(\mu(i_{k}(\mathbf{t}))+\mu(j_{k}(\mathbf{t})))\phi_{a_{k}(\mathbf{t})}

as
\mathbf{\tilde{t}}\in T(\sigma,\pi)
. Thus, from
{\bm{\beta}}
we obtained a unique
\bm{\phi}
satisfying (
20
). Conversely, notice that the relation (
21
) is bijective with inverse given by

\forall a\geq 2,\ \beta_{a}=\sum_{k=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{a+k}\binom{a-2}{k}\phi_{n-1-k}

Indeed,

\displaystyle\beta_{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{a+k}\binom{a-2}{k}\phi_{n-1-k}=\sum_{k=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{a+k}\binom{a-2}{k}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\beta_{j}\binom{k}{j-2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{n}(-1)^{a}\beta_{j}\sum_{k=j-2}^{a-2}(-1)^{k}\binom{a-2}{k}\binom{k}{j-2}=\sum_{j=2}^{n}(-1)^{a}\binom{a-2}{j-2}\beta_{j}\sum_{k=j-2}^{a-2}(-1)^{k}\binom{a-j}{k-j+2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{n}(-1)^{a+j}\binom{a-2}{j-2}\beta_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{a-j}(-1)^{k}\binom{a-j}{k}=\sum_{j=2}^{n}(-1)^{a+j}\binom{a-2}{j-2}\beta_{j}\delta_{a=j}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{2a}\binom{a-2}{a-2}\beta_{a}=\beta_{a}.

And,

\displaystyle\phi_{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\binom{n-a-1}{k-2}\beta_{k}=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\binom{n-a-1}{k-2}\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}(-1)^{k+j}\binom{k-2}{j}\phi_{n-1-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}\phi_{n-1-j}\sum_{k=2}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom{n-a-1}{k-2}\binom{k-2}{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}\binom{n-a-1}{j}\phi_{n-1-j}\sum_{k=2+j}^{n-a+1}(-1)^{k}\binom{n-a-1-j}{k-2-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n-a-1}{j}\phi_{n-1-j}\sum_{k=0}^{n-a-1-j}(-1)^{k}\binom{n-a-1-j}{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n-a-1}{j}\phi_{n-1-j}\delta_{j=n-a-1}=\binom{n-a-1}{n-a-1}\phi_{a}=\phi_{a}.

Notice this readily implies that through such
{\bm{\beta}}
given
{\bm{\phi}}
, we obtain that (
20
) holds.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9
.

It follows from Theorems
D.6
,
D.8
, and Proposition
1
, together with the observation that since
\mathrm{d}^{-\mu}_{-{\bm{\beta}}}={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}
, it is possible to cover all the possible semimetrics by restricting to
B_{n}^{\geq}{\mkern 0.0mu\times\mkern 0.0mu}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\geq}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
10
.

For all
j\in[2,n]
we have that
\beta_{j}=(-1)^{j}(\Delta^{j-2}\bm{\phi})_{n+1-j}\geq 0
. Indeed,

\displaystyle(-1)^{a}(\Delta^{a-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-a}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a}\sum_{i=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{a-2+i}\binom{a-2}{i}\phi_{n+1-a+i}=\sum_{i=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{i}\binom{a-2}{i}\phi_{n+1-a+i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{i}\binom{a-2}{a-2-i}\phi_{n+1-a+i}=\sum_{j=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{a-2-j}\binom{a-2}{j}\phi_{n+1-a+a-2-j}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a}\sum_{j=0}^{a-2}(-1)^{j}\binom{a-2}{j}\phi_{n-1-j}=\beta_{a}.

Now we prove that
(-1)^{j}(\Delta^{j-2}\bm{\phi})_{n+1-j-k}\geq 0
for any
k\in[0,n-j]
. We have already verified the basis case with
k=0
, thus now we assume this to hold for every
k\leq m
and we prove it for
m+1
. By a known fact
19
19

19

\displaystyle(\Delta^{k}f)_{j+1}-(\Delta^{k}f)_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i+k}\binom{k}{i}f(j+i+1)-\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i+k}\binom{k}{i}f(j+i)=-\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}(-1)^{i+k}\binom{k}{i-1}f(j+i)-\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i+k}\binom{k}{i}f(j+i)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}(-1)^{i+k}\left(\binom{k}{i}+\binom{k}{i-1}\right)f(j+i)=\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}(-1)^{i+k+1}\binom{k+1}{i}f(j+i)=(\Delta^{k+1}f)_{j}

, we have that

\displaystyle(\Delta^{j-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-m}-(\Delta^{j-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-(m+1)}

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{j+1-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-(m+1)}

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{j+1-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-(j+1)-m}.

Thus,

\displaystyle(\Delta^{j-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-(m+1)}=(\Delta^{j-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-m}-(\Delta^{j+1-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-(j+1)-m}

\displaystyle(-1)^{j}(\Delta^{j-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-(m+1)}=(-1)^{j}(\Delta^{j-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-j-m}+(-1)^{j+1}(\Delta^{j+1-2}{\bm{\phi}})_{n+1-(j+1)-m}.

The latter terms are both non-negative by inductive hypothesis.
Conversely, if
{\bm{\phi}}\in\Phi_{n}
is totally monotone, then (if
{\bm{\beta}}:=F^{-1}({\bm{\phi}})
)
\beta_{j}=(-1)^{j}(\Delta^{j-2}\bm{\phi})_{n+1-j}\geq 0
. Moreover, since
\beta_{2}=\phi_{n-1}>0
, we have that
{\bm{\beta}}\in B_{n}^{*}
.
∎

D.1.
A characterization of Axiom A.5 and A.6

A.7
.

There is a function
c:[n-1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{++}
such that for all
\sigma,\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
a,b\in[n-1]

(22)

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})\implies\frac{\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})}{\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})}=c(a,b).

Proposition D.9
.

A metric
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axioms A.5 and A.6 if and only if it satisfies Axiom A.7.

Notice that whenever such
c
exists by (
22
), we have
c(a,b)c(b,a)=1
for all
a,b\in[n-1]
, and in particular
c(a,a)=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axiom A.7. Let
\sigma,\pi,\rho,\tau
and
a
be such that
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})\sqcup I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})\sqcup I(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1})
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
, and
I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=I(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1})
. Since
c(a,a)=1
, we have
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
, and
\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1})
. Therefore, it holds
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1})
. Thus,
\mathrm{d}
satisfies Axiom A.6.

We now show that
\mathrm{d}
satisfies A.5. Let
\sigma,\pi,\rho,\tau
and distinct
a,b\in[n-1]
be s.t.
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})
and
I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})=I(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})
. By (
22
),

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})}{\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})}=c(a,b)=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})}{\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})}.

Assume now that
\mathrm{d}
satisfies both A.5 and A.6. We show that is satisfies Axiom A.7. Let
\rho,\tau
and distinct
a,b\in[n-1]
be s.t.
I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})=I(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})
. Define

c(a,b):=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})/\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})
. Now we show that
c
is well-defined. By Axiom A.5, for all
\pi,\sigma
such that
I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=I(\sigma,\sigma t_{b,b+1})
, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})}{\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})}=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})}{\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{b,b+1})},

and hence
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})/\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{b,b+1})=c(a,b)
.

We claim that A.6 implies that if
\sigma,\rho\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
, and
a\in[n-1]
are such that
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
, then
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
. Suppose
n\leq 3
. Then,
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
implies that
\sigma=\rho
; therefore, the result holds. So, assume
n\geq 4
. By contradiction, suppose, that
I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
but
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})\neq\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
. As
\mathrm{d}
is a metric, there exists
b\in[n-1]
such that
b\neq a
and
b\neq a+1
, and that
\sigma_{b}\neq\rho_{b}
. Clearly,
(\sigma_{b},\rho_{b})\notin I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})
. Let
\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
be such that
\pi_{a}=\sigma_{b}
,
\pi_{a+1}=\rho_{b}
, and
\tau=\pi
.
By construction, we have

I(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})\sqcup I(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=I(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})\sqcup I(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1}).

Therefore, by A.6,
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})+\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1}).
However, notice that, by construction of
\pi
,
\mathrm{d}(\pi,\pi t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\tau,\tau t_{a,a+1})
, which, in turn, implies
\mathrm{d}(\sigma,\sigma t_{a,a+1})=\mathrm{d}(\rho,\rho t_{a,a+1})
, a contradiction. Therefore, (
22
) holds.
∎

D.2.
Isometric embeddability

Proof of Proposition
12
.

We consider the following orders on
C=[n]
,

\displaystyle o_{123}

\displaystyle=(1,2,3,4,\ldots,n),\,\,\,\,\ \ \ \ o_{132}=(1,3,2,4,\ldots,n)

\displaystyle o_{213}

\displaystyle=(2,1,3,4,\ldots,n),\,\,\,\,\ \ \ \ o_{231}=(2,3,1,4,\ldots,n)

\displaystyle o_{312}

\displaystyle=(3,1,2,4,\ldots,n),\,\,\,\,\ \ \ \ o_{321}=(3,2,1,4,\ldots,n)

Assume there exists an isometric embedding
f:\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}\to E
, we denote the images of these rankings under the isometry
f
as
f_{ijk}=f(o_{ijk})
and we assume without loss of generality that
f_{123}=\mathbf{0}_{E}
.
We have the following betweenness relations

o_{123}-o_{213}-o_{231}-o_{321}-o_{312}-o_{132}-o_{123}-o_{213}.

Also notice that
o_{213}
is the only element between
o_{123}
and
o_{231}
;
o_{231}
is the only element between
o_{213}
and
o_{321}
;
o_{321}
is the only element between
o_{231}
and
o_{312}
;
o_{312}
is the only element between
o_{321}
and
o_{132}
;
o_{132}
is the only element between
o_{312}
and
o_{123}
;
o_{123}
is the only element between
o_{132}
and
o_{213}
. Thus, by Axiom
A.3
, we must have that

(23a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(o_{123},o_{213})+\mathrm{d}(o_{213},o_{231})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(o_{123},o_{231}),

(23b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(o_{213},o_{231})+\mathrm{d}(o_{231},o_{321})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(o_{213},o_{321}),

(23c)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(o_{231},o_{321})+\mathrm{d}(o_{321},o_{312})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(o_{231},o_{312}),

(23d)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(o_{321},o_{312})+\mathrm{d}(o_{312},o_{132})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(o_{321},o_{132}),

(23e)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(o_{312},o_{132})+\mathrm{d}(o_{132},o_{123})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(o_{312},o_{123}),

(23f)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(o_{132},o_{123})+\mathrm{d}(o_{123},o_{213})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}(o_{132},o_{213}).

Since
E
is strictly convex and because
o_{213}
satisfies (
23a
), we can find a
\lambda_{1}\in(0,1)
such that

f_{213}=\lambda_{1}f_{231}.

In particular, we must have that
\lambda_{1}=\mathrm{d}(o_{123},o_{213})/\mathrm{d}(o_{123},o_{231})
.
Similarly, since
o_{231}
satisfies (
23b
), we must have some
\lambda_{2}\in(0,1)
such that

f_{231}=f_{213}+\lambda_{2}(f_{321}-f_{213}).

In particular, we must have

\lambda_{2}=\mathrm{d}(o_{231},o_{213})/\mathrm{d}(o_{213},o_{321})
.
Together with the previous result,

f_{231}=\lambda_{1}f_{231}+\lambda_{2}(f_{321}-\lambda_{1}f_{231})

and thus

f_{231}=\frac{\lambda_{2}}{1-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}f_{321}=:\nu_{1}f_{321},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,f_{213}=\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{1-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}f_{321}=:\nu_{2}f_{321},

where
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in(0,1)
. Now, since
o_{321}
satisfies (
23c
), there is some
\lambda_{3}\in(0,1)
such that

f_{321}=f_{231}+\lambda_{3}(f_{312}-f_{231}).

Combining this with the previous relations,

f_{321}=(1-\lambda_{3})\nu_{1}f_{321}+\lambda_{3}f_{312}

\displaystyle f_{321}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{3}}{1-(1-\lambda_{3})\nu_{1}}f_{312}

\displaystyle f_{231}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{3}\nu_{1}}{1-(1-\lambda_{3})\nu_{1}}f_{312}

\displaystyle f_{213}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{3}\nu_{2}}{1-(1-\lambda_{3})\nu_{1}}f_{312}

\displaystyle=:\upsilon_{1}f_{312}

\displaystyle=:\upsilon_{2}f_{312}

\displaystyle=:\upsilon_{3}f_{312}

Notice that
\upsilon_{1},\upsilon_{2},\upsilon_{3}\in(0,1)
. Again, since
o_{312}
satisfies (
23d
), there is some
\lambda_{4}\in(0,1)
such that

f_{312}=f_{321}+\lambda_{4}(f_{132}-f_{321})

Combining this with the previous relations,

f_{312}=(1-\lambda_{4})\upsilon_{1}f_{312}+\lambda_{4}f_{132}

\displaystyle f_{312}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{4}}{1-(1-\lambda_{4})\upsilon_{1}}f_{132}=:\theta_{1}f_{132}

\displaystyle f_{321}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{4}\upsilon_{1}}{1-(1-\lambda_{4})\upsilon_{1}}f_{132}=:\theta_{2}f_{132}

\displaystyle f_{231}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{4}\upsilon_{2}}{1-(1-\lambda_{4})\upsilon_{1}}f_{132}=:\theta_{3}f_{132}

\displaystyle f_{213}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{4}\upsilon_{3}}{1-(1-\lambda_{4})\upsilon_{1}}f_{132}=:\theta_{4}f_{132}

Notice that
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3},\theta_{4}\in(0,1)
.
Finally, since
o_{132}
satisfies
(
23e
)
, there is some
\lambda_{5}\in(0,1)
such that

f_{132}=f_{312}+\lambda_{5}(f_{123}-f_{312})

Combining this with the previous relations,

f_{132}=(1-\lambda_{5})\theta_{1}f_{132}+\lambda_{5}f_{123},\ \ \ \ f_{132}=\frac{\lambda_{5}}{1-(1-\lambda_{5})\theta_{1}}f_{123}

But,
f_{123}=\mathbf{0}_{E}
yields
f_{132}=f_{312}=f_{321}=f_{231}=f_{213}=\mathbf{0}_{E}
contradicting the injectivity of
f
.
∎

E.
Proofs in §
5

Computations of Example
13
.

Let
n=3
,
V=((1,2,3),(3,1,2),(2,3,1))
,
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,0)
and
\mu_{3}>\mu_{1},\mu_{2}>0
. Let define
m:=2(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3})
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((1,2,3),V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((3,2,1),V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+2\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((3,1,2),V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((2,1,3),V)

\displaystyle=m+2\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((2,3,1),V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((1,3,2),V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+2\mu_{3}

Thus, the median is
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)=\{(2,3,1)\}
.

Now let
\sigma=(3,2,1)
, then
\sigma V=((3,2,1),(1,3,2),(2,1,3))
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((1,2,3),\sigma V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+2\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((3,2,1),\sigma V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((3,1,2),\sigma V)

\displaystyle=m+2\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((2,1,3),\sigma V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((2,3,1),\sigma V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+2\mu_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\beta}^{\mu}((1,3,2),\sigma V)

\displaystyle=m+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}

In this case the median is
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma V)=\{(1,3,2)\}\neq\{(2,1,3)\}=\sigma\{(2,3,1)\}=\sigma P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
. Also notice that in this case
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma V)=\{1\}\neq\{2\}=\sigma\{2\}=\sigma W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
14
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
be such that
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})\neq\emptyset
. We denote by
V
the concatenation
V:=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
. First of all notice that,

\displaystyle\min\limits_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V)=\min\limits_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left[{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{1})+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{2})\right]\geq\min\limits_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{1})+\min\limits_{\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,V_{2}).

Therefore, we have that
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})\subseteq P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V).
Conversely, suppose that
\sigma\notin P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})
. Then, it holds

(24)

{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V)={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{1})+{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{2})>\min\limits_{\omega\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\omega,V_{1})+\min\limits_{\pi\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,V_{2}).

Let
\pi\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})\neq\emptyset
. By (
24
), we have that
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V)>{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,V)
. Therefore,
\sigma\notin P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
. By contrapositive, this yields that
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})=P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
15
.

Suppose first that
\beta_{k}=0
, for all
2\leq k<n
. Fix two profiles of voters
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathbf{V}_{n}
with
W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})\neq\emptyset
. Let
c\in W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})
. Then, for all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
with
c\in M(C,\sigma)
, we have
\sigma\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})
. Indeed note that, as
c\in W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})
, there exist preferences
\pi^{1}\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})
and
\pi^{2}\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})
such that
c\in M(C,\pi^{1})
and
c\in M(C,\pi^{2})
. Given that
\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}=0
, we obtain, for
m=1,2
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}\left(\pi^{1},V_{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{|V_{m}|}\beta_{n}\mu\left(\{c\}\bigtriangleup M(C,v^{j})\right)={\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi^{2},V_{m}).

Thus,
\sigma\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})
and by Proposition
14
we have that
P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})=P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
. This yields

W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})=W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V).

Conversely, without loss of generality we can assume that
\mu
is the counting measure. We want to show that if
W_{{\bm{\beta}}}
satisfies reinforcing for winners, then
\beta_{k}=0
, for all
k\in[2,n-1]
. To this end suppose that
\beta_{k}>0
, for some
k\in[2,n-1]
. If
n=3
, we can directly skip to Paragraph
Final Computations
below. So, assume for the moment that
n\geq 4
. Consider the following two electorates
V_{1},V_{2}\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=((1,2,3,4,\ldots,n),(2,3,1,4,\ldots,n),(3,1,2,4,\ldots,n))

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=((1,2,3,4,\ldots,n),(2,1,3,4,\ldots,n)),

with
V
denoting their concatenation, i.e.
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
. We first show that if
\sigma\in P_{{\bm{\beta}}}(V_{1})
then it is without loss of generality to assume that
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}=\{1,2,3\}
.
Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
and suppose
\sigma_{1}\notin\{1,2,3\}
. Let
k^{*}=\min\{i\in[n]\mid\sigma_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}\}
. We have
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k^{*}-1},\sigma_{k^{*}},\sigma_{k^{*}+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n})
, and that
\sigma_{i}\in\{1,2,3\}
for some
i>k^{*}
. Let
\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
be defined as

\pi:=(\sigma_{k^{*}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k^{*}-1},\sigma_{k^{*}+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}).

Let
B\subseteq[n]
be such that
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{k^{*}}\}\subseteq B
and
|B|=k
. Define the following collections of sets

(1)

\mathcal{S}_{1}:=\{A\subseteq[n]\mid\sigma_{k^{*}},\sigma_{1}\in A\}

(2)

\mathcal{S}_{2}:=\{A\subseteq[n]\mid\sigma_{k^{*}}\in A,\sigma_{1}\notin A,\exists i\in[2,k^{*}-1],\;\textnormal{s.t.}\;\sigma_{i}\in A\},

and observe that
A\in 2^{[n]}\setminus(\mathcal{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{2})
if and only if
M(A,\sigma)=M(A,\pi)
. We have

(25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{1})-\mathrm{d}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,V_{1})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{|V_{1}|}\sum_{A\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{2}}\beta_{|A|}\left(|M(A,\sigma)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|-|M(A,\pi)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\sum_{j=1}^{|V_{1}|}\sum_{A\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\beta_{|A|}\left(|M(A,\sigma)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|-|M(A,\pi)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{|V_{1}|}\sum_{A\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\beta_{|A|}\left(2-|M(A,\pi)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}\sum_{j=1}^{|V_{1}|}\beta_{k}\left(2-|M(B,\pi)\triangle M(B,v^{j})|\right)\overset{(e)}{\geq}\beta_{k}(2-0)=2\beta_{k}>0,

where (
a
) holds because if
A\in 2^{[n]}\setminus(\mathcal{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{2})
, then
M(A,\sigma)=M(A,\pi)
. (
b
) holds because, if
A\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
, then
M(A,\sigma)>3
, and
M(A,v^{j})\leq 3
, for all
j\in[|V_{1}|]
. This implies that

|M(A,\sigma)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|-|M(A,\pi)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|=2-|M(A,\pi)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|\geq 0
, for all
j\in[|V_{1}|]
.
(
c
) holds because for all subsets
A\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
, it holds
M(\sigma,A)=\sigma_{1}
while
M(A,v^{j})\neq\sigma_{1}
, for all
j\in[|V_{1}|]
, which, in turn, implies that
|M(A,\sigma)\triangle M(A,v^{j})|=2
; (
d
) holds by definition of
B
; (
e
) holds because there is at least one voter
j
such that
M(B,v^{j})=M(B,\pi)=\sigma_{k^{*}}
.

This shows that any optimal permutation is such that
\sigma_{1}\in\{1,2,3\}
. There are two cases to be discussed. Let
\mathcal{K}:=\{k\in[2,n-1]:\beta_{k}>0\}
be the set of
\beta
that are positive.

Case 1.

Suppose there exists
k\in\mathcal{K}
such that
k\leq n-2
. One can remove
\sigma_{1}
from the alternative set, restrict
v^{j}
to
[n]\setminus\{\sigma_{1}\}
, and
{\bm{\beta}}
to
(\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{n-1})
(see Lemma
A.7
). By applying Lemma
A.7
, we conclude that any optimal permutation
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
is such that
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
.

Furthermore, we can repeat the process by restricting
v^{j}
to
[n]\setminus\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}
, and by truncating
{\bm{\beta}}
as
(\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{n-2})
. By applying again Lemma
A.7
and repeating a third time the same arguments, we obtain that if
\sigma\in P_{{\bm{\beta}}}(V_{1})
then
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}=\{1,2,3\}
. Now, fix
\sigma\in P^{\mu}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(V_{1})
. Construct
\pi
in the following way:
\pi_{1}=\sigma_{1}
,
\pi_{2}=\sigma_{2}
,
\pi_{3}=\sigma_{3}
, and
\pi_{i}=i
, for
i\geq 4
. Then, we have that that
\mathrm{d}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(\sigma,V_{1})\geq\mathrm{d}_{{\bm{\beta}}}(\pi,V_{1})
. Indeed, suppose that
A\in 2^{[n]}
, if
A\subseteq[4,n]
, for all
j\in[|V_{1}|]

|M(A,\sigma)\bigtriangleup M(A,v^{j})|\geq|M(A,\pi)\bigtriangleup M(A,v^{j})|=0.

If
A\cap\{1,2,3\}\neq\emptyset
, then
M(A,\sigma)=M(A,\pi)
and hence for all
j\in[|V_{1}|]
,

|M(A,\sigma)\bigtriangleup M(A,v^{j})|=|M(A,\pi)\bigtriangleup M(A,v^{j})|.

Therefore, there exists
\sigma^{*}\in P_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})
such that
\sigma^{*}_{i}=i
for
i\geq 4
and
\sigma^{*}([3])=\left\{1,2,3\right\}
.

Case 2.

Suppose that
\beta_{n-1}>0
and that
\beta_{k}=0
for
k\leq n-2
. The same steps underlying (
25
) plus an application of Lemma
A.7
imply that if
\sigma\in P_{{\bm{\beta}}}(V_{1})
then
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
. As all sets of size
n-1
contain at least one between
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
, alternatives in positions from
3
to
n
are irrelevant when comparing
\sigma
with
v^{j}
, for
j\in V_{1}
. Therefore, there exists
\sigma^{*}\in P_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})
such that
\sigma^{*}_{i}=i
for
i\geq 4
and
\sigma^{*}([3])=\left\{1,2,3\right\}
.

Similar arguments apply to the other electorates
V_{2}
and
V
. These steps imply that
W_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
,
W_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})\subseteq\{1,2\}
, and
W_{\bm{\beta}}(V)\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
.

Final Computations

Simple algebra yields that
W_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})=\{1,2,3\}
,
W_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})=\{1,2\}
, and hence
W_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{1})\cap W_{\bm{\beta}}(V_{2})=\{1,2\}
.
For
V
, we obtain

(26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}((1,2,3,4,\ldots,n),V)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{k=3}^{n}{\beta}_{k}\binom{n-3}{k-3}+10\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}{\beta}_{k}\binom{n-3}{k-2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}((2,1,3,4,\ldots,n),V)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{k=3}^{n}{\beta}_{k}\binom{n-3}{k-3}+12\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}{\beta}_{k}\binom{n-3}{k-2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\bm{\beta}}((2,3,1,4,\ldots,n),V)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{k=3}^{n}{\beta}_{k}\binom{n-3}{k-3}+14\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}{\beta}_{k}\binom{n-3}{k-2}.

Finally, as
\beta_{k}>0
, for some
k\geq 2
, we conclude that
2\notin W_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
, which shows that
W_{\bm{\beta}}
does not satisfy reinforcing for winners.
∎

Proof of Proposition
16
.

Let
V\in\mathbf{V}_{n}
and
\sigma^{*}\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
with
k\in M(C,\sigma^{*})
. If all voters in the profile
V
rank
k
as their most preferred alternative, there is nothing to prove. Up to relabeling, we can assume that
k>1
and that voter
v
of the profile
V
takes the form

1>_{v}2>_{v}\ldots>_{v}k-2>_{v}k-1>_{v}k>_{v}k+1>_{v}\ldots>_{v}n.

Without loss of generality we can take an upranking
V^{k}
obtained by substituting
v
with
w:=t_{k-1,k}v
. If we show that
k\in W_{\bm{\beta}}^{\mu}(V^{k})
, then the proof is concluded by a straightforward induction argument. For all linear orders
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, we have

•

for all
i\in\left[1,k-2\right]\cup\left[k+1,n\right]
,
i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,v}=i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,w}
,

•

for
i=k-1
,
(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap(k-1)^{\downarrow,v}=(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap\left((k-1)^{\downarrow,w}\cup\left\{k\right\}\right)
which in turn yields:

\left|(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap(k-1)^{\downarrow,v}\right|=\left|(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap(k-1)^{\downarrow,w}\right|+\mathbf{1}_{(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}}(k),

•

for
i=k
,
{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}={k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap\left({k}^{\downarrow,w}\setminus\left\{k-1\right\}\right)
which in turn yields:

\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|=\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,w}\right|-\mathbf{1}_{k^{\downarrow,\sigma}}(k-1).

Fix a linear order
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. The previous observations yield that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)\mu(i)=

\displaystyle f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)\mu(k)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap(k-1)^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)\mu(k-1)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in\left[1,k-2\right]\cup\left[k+1,n\right]}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(i)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)\mu(k)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|(k-1)^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap(k-1)^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(k-1)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in\left[1,k-2\right]\cup\left[k+1,n\right]}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(k)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(k)+f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)\mu(k)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\in\left[1,k-1\right]\cup\left[k+1,n\right]}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|k^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\right)\mu(k)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|i^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap i^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\mu(i).

The inequality above follows from the observations in the bullet points and Lemma
A.4
. It is important to notice that whenever
k\in M(C,\sigma)
, the above inequality holds as equality. To ease the notation we let, for all linear orders
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
and profile
U\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
,

h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,U):=\sum_{j=1}^{|U|}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[f_{\bm{\beta}}\left({i}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\right)-2f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{i}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{i}^{\downarrow,u^{j}}\right|\right)\right]\mu(i).

For all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, define
g(\sigma):=2\left(f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)\right)\mu({k})
. The inequality provided above yields

(27)

h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V^{k})\geq h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,{V})-g(\sigma)

for all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Consequently, it follows that

(28)

\displaystyle\min_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V^{k})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left[h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,{V})-g(\sigma)\right]\geq\min_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,{V})-\max_{\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}}g(\sigma).

Claim
1
.

Let
\pi\in\argmin_{\sigma}h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)-g(\sigma)
be such that
k\in M(C,\pi)
. Then,
\pi\in\argmin_{\sigma}h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V^{k})
.

Proof.

For all
\sigma\in\mathop{}\!\mathbb{S}_{n}
, by (
27
),

h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\pi,V)-g(\pi)\leq h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)-g(\sigma)\leq h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V^{k}).

Given that
k>_{\pi}{j}
, for all
j\neq k
, we have
h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\pi,V)-g(\pi)=h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\pi,V^{k})
.
∎

Now, observe that, by (
28
), whenever the intersection of
\argmin_{\sigma}h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)
and
\argmax_{\sigma}g(\sigma)
is non-empty, any element in this intersection minimizes
h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\sigma,V)-g(\sigma)
. Further, we have the following

\displaystyle\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap k^{\downarrow,w}\right|

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap\left({k}^{\downarrow,v}\cup\{k-1\}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left({k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right)\cup\left({k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap\{k-1\}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|+\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap\{k-1\}\right|,

where (
a
) holds by construction of
w
and (
b
) holds because
{k}^{\downarrow,v}\cap\{k-1\}=\emptyset
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,w}\right|\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|+\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap\{k-1\}\right|\right)

\displaystyle-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|+1\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,\sigma}\cap{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|+1\right)-f_{\bm{\beta}}\left(\left|{k}^{\downarrow,v}\right|\right),

where (
c
) and (
d
) hold because of Lemmas
A.3
and
A.4
, respectively. Observe that the upper bound is achieved by any linear order
\sigma
that ranks
k
as first alternative. Thus, any linear order that ranks
k
as its most preferred alternative maximizes
g
.

By assumption,
\sigma^{*}
minimizes
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\cdot,V)
(and so
h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\cdot,V)
) and it ranks
k
as its most preferred alternative. Therefore, it minimizes
h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\cdot,V)-g(\cdot)
(and so
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\cdot,V)-g(\cdot)
). By invoking Claim
1
, we conclude that
\sigma^{*}
is a minimizer of
h^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(\cdot,V^{k})
and, in turn, of
{\mathrm{d}^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}}(\cdot,V^{k})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
17
.

Let
V=(v^{1},\ldots,v^{m})\in{\mathbf{V}_{n}}
and
c\in[n]
, be such that
n(V,c)\geq 0
and that
c\notin W^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
. Without loss of generality we set
c=1
. Consider following sets

S=\left\{i\in[m]:c\in M\left([n],v^{i}\right)\right\}\ \textnormal{and}\ T=[m]\setminus S.

By assumption
|S|\geq|T|
. We have that for all
\omega\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
, candidate
c
is not ranked first. Let
\omega\in P^{\mu}_{\bm{\beta}}(V)
, we can, without loss of generality, write it as
